The :; ith annual general meeting of the Pearson and Knowles Ccal and Iron Compony,Umited,was held at the Company's otidc,es, Warrington, on Monday, for the pur- pose of receiving and pasing the accounts for the year ending dune 3Cth last, and for transacting the general business of the company. Mr. J. S. Harmood Banner, M.P. (chairman of the Board of Directors) presided, and there were also prcsent : Messrs. John J. Bleckly, Herbert J. Bleakly, R. Brancker, W. Peter Ry!ands (directors), James Dodds- (secretor)), John.Knowlcs, John Temple, Charles E. Bunch, John Bleckly, jun., W. Richardsan, D. Garnett and J. Paul Rylands (share- holders).

THE CHAIRMAN'S REVIEW.

===== 0002601_19091002_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
The CHAIRNIAN, in presenting the accounts and reports (which have already appeared in the" Examiner") said he would first like to express the regret of the DirectUrs that their colleague, Mr. NV. H. Meekly, was not able to be present that day. (Hear, hear). lie was unfortunately taken ill immediately after their last Board Meeting, and ha purposed gaing away on a holiday, his doctors not consenting to allow him to be present that day. He (the Chairman) thought the share- holders would be satisfied with the dividend they paid and also Ns ith the accounts which they presented, show- ing, as they did, the .same care end regard to the future Interests of the Company in the, Reserves and Depreciation they provided that they had done in past years. They would observe on look- ing at the accounts that the loans had been reduced by £36,575. Creditors were reduced Ay £3,200 ; nhilst the Reserve Fund was increased by £20,000. On the other side of the account they have pursued the policy, as regarded land, plant, Ste. of writing off all additions: though he might say dust they had made considerable additions to their property so that that item stood that day at £550,610 against £559,531 last year and £570,347 the previous year. They were pursuing the policy of continuously writing down that value. The Investment Account showed an addition of £8,60, which was caused by further invest- ments they bad had to make in taking up the Deben- ttires of the Moss Hall Coal Company ; and they would observe—which was rather a curiosity of finance—that that Investment Account exceeded the amount of their Reserve Fund, so that they had teken out of their general assets £2D,500 for purposes of invest- ments they had found it desirable to make. In consequence of the lamentable accident at the May- pole Colliery which happened in August, 1908, they had had to finance the Moss Hall Coal Company, and their debt to them this year—that was at June 30th last— was about £22,000 larger than on the 30th June, 1908. He would have to refer later to the Maypole accident, and to the future finance in relation thereto. As regarded the profits made, the total for this year was £61,890, against the profit in 1903 of 2116,010, and in 1907 of £110,680. Both departments, iron and coal, looking at the state of trade, had done reasonably well; but naturally it was a great disappointment to them that this year they had radaced the profits from the two previous years, and he regretted that business in the present year had not so far led to the expectation that they would renew the profits of either 1907 or 19198. The total sales of their own ad affiliated departments in the ircn trade for lest year showed tales of £1,352,781, and of coal, £590,351. That was a reduction frcra the totals of 1908 by a considerable sum, the totals for this year being £1,943,132 against £2,419,435 of 1908, the tonnage production for similar periods being, ccal ira,n, combined, in 1909, 1,368,752 tons against 1,733,5(4 tons in 1908. There was one point he would-like particularly to emphasise—that the dividend of 4,1 per cent. which thee declared for this year dis- tributed a total of .240,2f0. The lest year's distribution was £80,587 ; the 1907 distribution was £75,250. Their total wages in all departments during 1909 paid to workmen amounted to £620,956, and looking at the view so strongly eapressed by Labour leaders of the unequal share in THE DISTRie,UTION OF PROFITS -BETWEEN CAPITAL AND LABOUR, he would ask them seriously to consider whether the addition of the whole of the profits distributed to sl-are- holders, v.iaich they had given as a sins of £40,220 would have made a serious addition to the wages of the workmen, or whether there was much to he said for a contention of unfairness in distributing the nett pro- ceeds of manufacture as to £620,900 to workmen and to capital .240,220. As they knew, they had an engineering branch which did quiet work, still a very useful work, towards the profits of the concern. The total sees for last year amounted ,to £225,557. As regarded their general iron work, there had been con- siderable difficulty in obtaining sufficient prices, but the combination with Messrs. Rylands Brothers, Limited, which was now an affiliated concern to them, by Ovine' them a constant custom and customer for certain branches of their work, had helped considerably for the benefit of themselves and their workmen in retaining trade. He would not like to let that occasion pass without mentioning one or two advantages and (difficulties which they were under .in the conduct of

THEIR IRON TRADE. In Australia it hid teen for some time impossible for British manufacturers to import certain manufactures affectink their production, as the Germans were always prepared to do the trade at excessively- low prices, assisted by the bounty s3stem and by their desire to get rid of surpins stocks. In the autumn of 1908 the Australian Government resolved that a bounty should be given to Australian manufacturers in respect of goods manufactured from Austral7an cr British material and in consequence a considerable advantage had accruel to British trade, for which they all, both work- men and employers, should be grateful to the Australian Government. .(Hear, hear.) On the other band, they had still to face a considerable competition in India from the Continent and America' who, whilst paying none of the heavy taxcs incurred by British manufac- tnrerers in respect of the Navy and Army, which was kept up for the defence of India amongst others, tad yet the free right and use of the Indian market for the sale of their wares, and had run us very hard, assisted by the bounty system and their desire to sell surplus- stock for which they had no market in their own country. In South Africa, also, all orders for certain classes of manufactures required for railway extension were placed in the hands of British manufacturers. Under the new policy of the present Commcnwealth, the whole of the buying for the Transvaal, and possibly the Orange River Colony, was dealt with in Africa, and the foreign manufacturer, who had contributed nothing towards the cost of the war, nor shared in the lament- able sacriaces which this country bed to make in order to retain possession of the South African Colonies' was able to oatain tha orders fr r trade which would have afforded thousands of pounds in wages to British work- ing men. As regarded ,

THE COAL TRADE

it. had been worked—Wilder-very great difficulties during the past year. In the first place prices had been con- tinuously falling, in consequence of the extreme depres- sion in the shipping trade, and also the home trade, and the Miners' Eight Hours Bill, the Cbeckweightman's Bill, and other measures which bad been passed by Parliament had rendered it extremely difficult to know how to meet the additional cost in working, and also to retain the production which was necessary in order to keep the cost of a colliery at a low level. He (the Chairman) would not refer to the output of Moss Hall Ccal ComPany, but in their own collieries of Pearson and lino :ries tlle output last year was considerably less than the output of the previous year, and this year the output was likely still further to decline, and conse- quently the cost of coal getting to rise. It might interest them to know that, based on actual figures since the SOth June last, the output of coal at the various collieries varied from S to 11 per cent. less than the ordinary basis of last year. Fortunately for the consumer trade was bad this year, and therefore prices bad not risen ; hot the consumer might take it that immediately trade revived, that shortness of coal, in which he thought they were quite on a par with their fellows in South Wales, Scotland and other places, would tell upon the quantities of coal available, and

COAL WOULD RISE UNDER THE EIGHT HOURS BILL quicker and sconer than it would if that Bill bad not been passed. (Hear, hear.) It was impossible to pass legislation of that description without the consumer having to pay for it. There was one other fact in con- nection with the Mine:s' Eight flows Bill which was of interest, and that wes from their ow n figures they found that the miner was receiving—notwithstanding the fact that wages rates were not reduced less in wages than before the Eight Hours Bill was passed.He believed that was a raoder.ite amount, and in South Wales and other places the reduction in wages received by the miner was considerably higher. That was a very serious thing for the miners, I and he sympathized with them, .as he was sure they all did, in this serious reduction n their wages ; but at the present price of coal and in the difficulties under which the getting of coal was now carried on, hg thought he was right in saying that the sale price of coal was below the cost of production. He was sure they would all sympathiza with their Directors and with their managers especially both at Wigan and at Moss Hell, in the great extra labour they bad had to undergo this year. (Hear, hea-.) In the first place they had had the anxiety of this' new legislation ; in the next place they had hld to face the consequences of the accident at the Maypole, to which all their talent had been applied as to how to put out the fire, re-open the shafts and renew the getting of coal. They bad also had to face the Press comments in relation to that fire, and a long and prolonged inquest which was held in connection there- with. The accident at Maypole happened at a time when they had entered on a career of importance at that colliery, based on their expectations of profits ' which were, at the time of the accident, being fully

- Mr. WILLIAM RICHARDSON seconded, and the motion was carried. THANKING THE CHAIRMAN.

Mr. J. J. BLF,crer.v, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Harrnood Banner for his services in the cLair, said as his (the speaker's) brother had said, they were proud to follow such a chief. They were sorry that his sound financial principles did not commend themselves to the House of Commons. They were sorry for the liouse of Commons that it could not produce any one with financial principles equal to what they could find in that room.

Mr. BRANCKER seconded, and the motion was heartily agreed to. The CHAIRMAN in reply, said he knew they bed their difficulties to face, but they were encouraged by the shareholders to exhibit the greatest activity. They hid first-rate officials to take charge of their business both here and at Messrs. Rylands Bros. and at Wigan. Without them the Chßirman and the Board would not be able to facs their difficulties.

realized. Their managers, therefore, had bad to put in a new electrical plant, a new screening apparatus and a new washery for coal, which would have cost before they were finished over <£50,000, and to superintend and look after the erection of the same, notwithstanding the fact that owing to the accident the collieries were idle. The work could not he postponed, as it was already ordered, and when completed would render the Moss Hall a splendidly equipped colliery. (Hear,hear). Undoubtedly there was some improvement in the ircn trade, .which he hoped n ight continue, and coal he trusted, might follow. He could only add that their managing directors and their managers, both at Warring- 'ten and Wigan, including those at the Moss Hall Colliery Company, gave great attention and industry to the care of their interests. (Hear, hear.) He moved that the report and accounts be adopted. _ Mr. JOHN J. BLECKLY seconded. Mr. TEMPLE, in supporting, said the Eight Hours Bill was in his opinion, going to add considerably to the cost of all-inanufactures. The iron works were hit now; the Bill would hit them worse, and hit every industry in Ih3 country. .

THE DIVIDEND.

The CHAIRMAN moved: "That the usual dividend at the rate of six per cent. per annum on the Prefer- ence Shares, and a dividend also at the rate of six per cent. per annum on the ordinary shares both less tax, and for the half-year ended 3Gth June fast, he paid on and after the 29th instant." _

Mr. J. J. BLECKLY seconded, and said he did so with mixed feelings—cne of disappointment, the other of satisfaction. It wag, of course, disappointing, after what they did last year, to come down to 4 per cent. dividend. It would have teen very much pleasant; f to him to have proposed end for them to have sanc- tioned a dividend of a higher amount. However, he thought that in the circumstances which the Chair- man had explained, they might be satisfied that they had come out as well as they had done. (Hear, hear). They had had a bad year, and, in common with other people in their trade, had suffered from the general depressicu. They could legitimately congratulate themselves upon the policy of investing surplus funs in sutsidiary companies. These were their best customers and contributed also their full share to the dividends. He referred last year to the Woikfran's Compensation Act—that was the amended Act, 1.006;-- and would like to say now that the more they experi- enced the working of it the more they were convinced that its tendency was not only to put unnecessarily onerous burdens cn themselves, hut to demoralize the workmen in several directions ; and he was glad to see an article in one of the papers giving expression to similar views from a working man's standpoint. The Eight Hours Act was aimed at the coal owner, but he took comfort to himself that it would hit someone else. There was no mistake about it that the public I must ultimately pay the piper—the Bill in its opera- tion was equivalent to putting into their trade o new customer who would take ten per cent. of their output, as it would reduce production to that extent. Ele would like again to draw attention to the menacing position brought about by the recent alliances formed amongst the principal Railway Companies, which were in his opinion fraught with great danger to the trading community. The President of the Board of Trade should be their protector in this matter, but he was afraid the present occupant of that position was too much engaged in other directions to tackle such a question as this, even if he htd the experience and capacity to do so—which was not at all certain. As regards the prospects of trade they were anxiously looking for a rift in the clouds, and there were some signs of improvement which he he hoped would develop into good trade by and by. Meanwhile one could not help casting somewhat envious eyes on the state of abounding prosperity which our confreres in America were enjoying. To be sure we were told that the fiscal policy which produced this state of things was stupid and barbarous, born of ignor- ance rnd confusion of thought, but if in contrast with this the fact that oar own more scientific and enlightened theories produced only unemployment and a diminuticn of wows and business, one was a little disposed to agree with Lord Rosebery that there might be worse things than Tariff Reform as an alternative. (An.plause). .

CHILLY OCTOBER NIGHTS.

How to Escape Throat and Chest Troubles.

With the arrival of " chill October " comes the beginning of the bad weather season, when the cold rain and winds bring in their train,coughs, colds, sore throats, bronchitis, and other all too common ailments of the throat and chest. To keep Peps handy is the surest and safeguard against the perils of chilly October nights. Few people even to-day realise how serious a tfiatter the catching of a cold or chill may be, although very many sufferers from chronic bronchitis and asthma owe their trouble to neglect when • catching cold through getting wet, or getting a chill on the chest because of the sudden weather changes. The discovery of Peps, however, opens the path of deliver- ance even to the _chronic sufferer,

Peps are a unique concentration of the particular natural substances which Nature has fulaished for the cure of human throat and chest trouble, and their action embodies Nature's own way to the lungs. As a Peps tablet dissolves in the mouth, the curative fumes which it emits are inhaled with the breath. They bathe the air-passages and have the most sooth- ing and healing influence. Liquid cough-mixtures are merely swallowed into the stomach. and conse- quently do not reach the lungs at all. Peps are unique in that they go direct to the seat of throat and chest disease.

Peps alSo contain no opium, chloral, nor any of the other harmful drugs to be found in cough-mixtures and cheap lozenges. Keep your box of Peps always handy--by your &Aside for that troublesome night cough. A course of these splendid tablets will entirely cure bronchitis and prevent a return of those attacks that have been getting worse year by year. Sold by all chemists, Is. I Id. or 2s. 9d. per box.

SPORT AND OUTDOOR LIFE.

There are over a score of splendid stories and articles in the Ow.ober of" Fry's Magazide," and there is not one of them that does not well repay reading. Every page is replete with interest, and among its varied list of contents are to be found articles on such engrossing subjects as "The Lady and the Motor," by Dorothy Levitt; " Best Games of Patience"; "The Partridge"; " Incidents in Tuna Fishing"; "The Vellman Airship"; "The Invincible rrout-"; "The Harvest Rabbit"; " A Winning Hazard Machine" ;• "The Sporting Side of Motor Cycling"; -1' Golf for Duffers"; and many other subjects of ever-topical interest. In addition- to the above this excellent issue also contains fine sporting stories by N. Alexander, J. M. Dedington, &c., while there is an absorbing article on "The 1-kn. George Lambton at Stanley House, Newmarket," and another, in the series on "The Sporting Estates of England," on "The Grange, Arlesford."

SCOTLAND'S RECORD OATS CROP.

Scotch farmercare able to congratulate themselves upon the excellence of this year's oats. So far as quality is concerned the oats that have just been harvested are reported to be the very finest that have, been produced within the memory of man. Messrs. R. Robinson and Sons, the wellknown millers of ALnan, state that throughout their many years experience they have never seen oats as flue as those being sold to-day„ by the Scottish farmers. The oats are remarkable for their size, richness, colour and flavour. In whatever other "fields the foreigner may triumph it is evident that Scotland still lead's the world se-far as oats are concerned.

Mr. GEORGE BALL, jun., proposed the toast of "The President and Vice-Presidents." He said Captain Crosfield had been president of the Association from its formation and they highly appreciated his services in that position. The Association had also reason to be thaukf id that it had such splendid vice-presidents who had given its work a decided fillip. The CHAIRMAN, in responding, ssid he was sorry he was unable to be present at' the final. He was going in for a competition himself and he was proud to say he was able to do something for the credit of old Warring. tin by carrying off the officers' jumping prize for the whole of the West Lancashire division. (Applause.) From the secretary's report, there was no doubt they had had a record year, twenty-five works having sent thirty-five teams. Captain Crosfield went on to enter- tain the company with an amusing story of War Office punctiliousness, with special reference to the use of decimals, and excused himself from embarking upon any dissertation on bowls by remarking that his time had been fully occupied that week-end in com- posing a stinging reply to his brother's letter in the "Examiner" on the subject of invasion. (Laughter.) He wished the Association all success in the coming year. He congratulated -them till, especially the com- mittee and secretary, on the splendid way in which they had worked. He believed they had had not less than eighteen meetings ranging from eight o'clock in the evening until any time up to three or four in the morning. (Laughter.) Their labours were Herculean, and he hoped all the members felt grateful to them, (Applause). A. H. CROSPIELD, M.P., fig one of the riec- presidents, also responded. Ile said his brother had been telling them a story about decimals. (A Voice : "Tell us something about the Budget.") No, there were to be no politics that night. (Laughter.) He heard on very good authority that /within our own time a distinguished Chancellor of the Exchequer, when he went to his office and had a look through the figures, came across a number of calculations in decimals. He looked at them for a time and then said : Confound it all! What do all those infernal little dots mean?" (Langhter.) That was not the present Chancellor of the Exchequer—(langhter)s—but it was one in cur own time. Continuing Mr. Crosfield said it gave him pa,ticular pleasure to come to a festive gathering of that kind, the more so because very old and very good friends of his were there also—gentle- men of a different political colour to his own but none the less good friends all the same. (Applause). He was happy to join with them in congratulating the association upon the remarkably successful season they had had, because he was quite sure there was not one of them whatever his views might he on other questions, %Ito did not rejoice from every point of view to see the wonderful development of that healthy, capital, accessible form of outdoor recreation which they had in Warrington in the goodsold English game of bowls. (Applause). He would not trouble them with a lot of ancient quotations on the game, although they made interesting reading. It was, however,worthy of note that in the days of the Edwards the Richards, and other bygone monarchs, regulations were issued forbidding the people to play the game of bowls because it had made such head- way, even in those days—although they had only stone discs which would not snit his friend Mr. Ball nowadays, and the game had to be gradually evolved—that it was actually interfering with the old English and very necessiry practice of archery. Therefore regulations were issued, which he was happy to think there would be no need to repeat in our own time. We did not live for bowls or every other game, for games were all a means to an end, and as his brother bad touched on a highly debatable topic he (the speaker) would take the opportunity of saying that he hoped and believed that none of the keenness of sportsmen on their games would interfere with the readiness of Englishmen to support the great Terri- torial institution to nhich they all necessarily attached such•very great importance. (Applause). The evolu- tion of bowls was a very interesting subject, beginning as it did in a small and humble way with the throwing of the stone disc. We had seen something of that evolution in our own time and even in our own town, for the development of the game in the course of tbe last . fifteen years had been really splendid. (Hear, bear.) In common with the Chairman,he congratulated that gathering most heartily on the striking success of the Works Bowling 'Associa- tion. He felt grestly struck, as he was sure they all did, with the admirable report .of the Secre'ary, and not the least proud to think of the really good spirit and friendly feeling with which those great competi- tions were fought out. (Applause). Mr. R. PIERPOMT also responded. He congratulated the Asseciation sincerely on the work it hsd done that year and in the years gone by. What he particularly admired about the game of bowls was the fact that it was a game played by individuals for the individual honour. They were each striving as honour:lbl° gentle- men to arrive at a certain point of excellence in the game. He had nothing to say about the system of other games, but be delighted in the idea that, if he might use an Ithhism' a man might play bowls off his own bat end work for himself, instead of merely looking upon others working. WORKS BOWLING ASSOCIATION.

Annual Prize Distribution.

PRESENTATION TO R. J. WILLETT.

The Warrington Works Bowling Association has just concluded a record seavn and on Monday evening the members assembled in the club room attached to the Bowling Green Inn, Bank Quay, for the annual prize distribution. The gathering was undoubtedly the most successful in • the history of this flowishing association, the proceedirgs being characterised by a spirit of ccrdial good fellowship betweri those who haxe during the summer months engaged in keen but friendly rivalry on the greer. A number of prominent supporters of the Association were present and inter- esting speech-making was pleasantly interspersed with song. Captain G. R. Crostield (president of the association) was in the chzir and he . was accompanied at the cross taLle by the Alnyi r (Alderman Vorshaw), Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., .Nr. A. H. Crosfleld, Mr. Robert Pierpoint, Councillors Fulton-Smith, Locker, Broadhurst and Wilson, Mr. J. Dodds, Mr. J. Willett (chairman of the committee), and Mr. W. Patrick (hon. secretary). The toast of "The King " was enthusiastically honoured on the propEsition of the chairman. THE ANNUAL REPORT.

Mr. PATRICK (the secretary of the sescciation), in his annual report Stated that this season saw the close of the associatiou's apprenticeship it having teat in existence for seven years. On March IGth the annual meeting was held, and 15 works were represented. Aftey all the officers and committee had been appointed for the season 1909 the most important busineas came fornatd, namely, the recommendation of the com- mittee for 1908, to handicap tams entering the com- petition for 1909. A lengthy and somewhat heated discussion followed, and eventually it was proposed and seconded " That we adopt the suggestion of the committee," thirteen voting for the handicap and two against; As a result of that discussion, a record of every description had been broken, They commenced with bad entry of 35 teams, representing 25 works, which was the largest entry they had ever had. No fewer than 744 names were registered from the different works in connection with the competition. Thirty-six games were played before they reached the final, and twelve Satordays were required in which to play the games. Even the clerk of the weather smiled on their efforts and favoured them with fine weather all through. The day on Ilia the final was played was the finest Saturday of all. The only regret they had on that day v.as that their President cauld not be with them. Another regret was that they could not accommodate more spectators. They had at least 1,000 round the green on that day, but many more were tinned away because they could not find them room. It was a pity, but now the necessity of more accommodation bad been proved they might possibly get it in the future. The final game ended in Rylands' No. 1 defeating Whitecross by 16 points. The competition finished, they entered upon the annual handicap lyith 274 entries (another record). This took five Saturdays to compete, and the object in gathering that night wts to present the prizes to the winners' 69 in all—another record—and when he told them that in round figures they were giving nearly £4O in prizes, and that from entry fees they received £l3 14s. and £5 55., so kindly given by Lady G'reenall for the first prize, they would see that it had been no small task on the part of the committee to get the money together. A word of praise was due to players and spectators alike, for the admir- able way in which they had conducted themselves. On no single occasion had it been necessary for Mr. Car-apt:ell to complain of the cenduct on the green, neither bad it been necessary for the committee to yarn anyone. That spoke well for the association. (Applause). . "THE PRESIDENT AND VICE-PRESIDENT."

They had got to know their land and their lengths and if they were strikers they must have good aim to knock their opponent out. WARRINGTON CRICKET LEAGUE. Presentation of Shield and Medals,

For the second year in succession St. James' Cricket Club have had the honour of winning the Silver Challenge Shield presented to the Warrington and Dis- trict Cricket League by Colonel J. Reid Walker and on Tuesday evening last they received it at the hands of Councillor J. Fulton-Smith at a social gathering in the Crown and Septre Inn Buttermarket-street. Councillor Fulton-Smith also presented the members of St. James' team with the gold medals of the League and the gold centred silver medals to Rylands' Recreation Club the runners-up.

Mr. J. F. FOSTER (captain of St. James') presided read letters of apology for non-attendance from Colonel J. Reid Walker, tho Rev. H. Gray and Mr. W. P. Rylands. Continuing be said that Councillor Fulton-Smith was practically deputising for Colonel Reid Walker who was unavoidably detained elsewhere. He was also pleased to see Mr. E. Draper who had come to assist in the ceremony. It aas . very gratifying to them to have those two gentlemen with them that evening, because it showed that interest was taken in League and in outdoor sport generally. Both of them were very keenly interested in all things appertaining to outdoor sport and anything for the recreation of young men. (Applause). Councillor FeLTON-SMITH then presented the medals. He also handed a gold medal to Mr. J. H. Constable for the best batting average and a gold centre medal to Mr. G. McGowan for bowling which were presented to the League by Mr. George Eastwood. He said that he was sorry that Col anel Reid Walker had not been able to be present. He had sant a very kind message to them and he hoped they would all be very successful and united in their future success. (Applause.) There had been much said about sport recently because it was uppermost in many people's mind 3. If it were not cricket it was football, and if it were not football it was tennis or something else. There was a general concenses of opinion that there was no life without sport and he dared say that there was a great deal of truth in it. that. That was one of the means to which men naturally resorted to recuperate their health end strength for their daily work. No matter what direction their work might every one required some change of occupation so that his muscles and all nis tissues might have some rest in each department. Sport was coming to be recognised now af to be one of the important fjements in our social life. Besides the physical improvements which good sport, well directed, secured, there was the social side. He maintained that sport promoted self-control. He then presented the shield to Mr. Forster. Mr. FortsTEß replying said they had received three applications to join the League and there was only one vacancy. They would'either have to have two divisions or only accept one of the applications. Mr. E. DRSPER said that the League was giving a considerable impeteS to the game. To be brought within the influence of cricket was a great boon to anybody. Mr. FOSTER moved a vote of thanks to Councillor Fulten-Smith and Mr. Draper. Mr. NEWTON, captain, of Rylands team, seconded. A vote of thanks Was accorded to Mr. Eastwood for presenting the two medals. At the conclusion of the presentation-ceremony a smoking concert was held, during which •an excellent programme was submitted.—The following rendered songs :—Messrs. Turner, Gelderd, J. Lloyd, Beardsley, Lonadale and Whitfield. Mr. Roberts played two pianoforte solos.

STRAIGHT WORDS FROM A JUDGE. Kildare, bat it seemed to be a feeling amongst' jurymen that when they entered the box they ought to hang up their brains with their hats and become drivelling idiots.

PRESENTATION TO MASTER AND MATRON OF DUTTON WORKHOUSE.

Mr. and Mrs. Ti'. D. Rollinson (master and matron of Dutton Workhouse) are relinquishing their positions to take up the duties of master and matron at Waver- ' hampton Workhouse. On Tuesday a number of members of the Runcorn Board of Guardians assembled to make a small presentation as a token of their friendship and esteem. The gift took the form of a silver cake dish with an incription which read as follows : "Presented to Mr. and Mrs. Rollinson by a number of memhers of the Board of Guardians as a token of esteem in which they were held as Master and Matrsn at the Runcorn Union Workhouse, September, 1909:". Mr. T. Baxter, chairmen of the Board, made the presentation. WARRINGTON ANGLERS ASSOCIATION.

Juvenile Contest.

PRESERVING FRUIT.

At this season, when the orchard is being rifled el its products, it is important to consider how fruit may be best preserved for winter use. There are several ways of doing this. It may be made into some kind of jam or jelly for which there is a continual demand during the months when fresh fruit is Lot available. Dry or evaporated fruits are more used for food to-day than formerly, and tinned fruits are also in demon. But perhaps the best method of preserving fruit fresh for winter use is by bottling, a means which the French have adopted with considerable success. Bottling is the easiest and most convenient method of preserving fruit. In the English farmhouse it is most commonly applied to plums. Of late years there has bcan a tendency to simplify the method of preserving. Formerly preserves according to the older recipes were more like sweetmeats than fruit, so liberal wss the amount of sugar employed. To-day the best are kept simply by means of hot water. Plums that have been simply and adequately treated can be cooked at Christ- n:ais, and the most fastidious testa will be unable to discover any difference between them and those which were cooked straight off the tree in September.— Country Life."

Scotland's Triumph

This season the oats of Scotland have beaten all records for richness, colour and flavour. Never has Nature been kinder in providing the crop—never has a more successful harvest gladdened .the Scotch. farmer's heart.

orthodox. Wrigley, though apparently out of con- dition, played a great centre game, and the wings are both strong and speedy. The team that wins at Fartown this season can pat itself on the back. A good many of them will do worse than Warrington did.

The enthusiasm for the game in the Yorkshire town is something to marvel at. There were 12,000 people at Monday's match, and 'lO,OOO on Tuesday when Hull bit the dust, and the crowds are obviously chock full of confidence in their team. Warrington's appearance on the field was signalised by the releasing of a balloon in the Huddersfield colours, and the waving of a huge flag from the ranks of the popular side. While to cap all, some perfervid Fartowner released a tiny black kitten in the field of play as an omen of good luck. Even the kitten had a Huddersfield ribbon on. They mean to make things hum in Huddersfield this season, I .can tell you !

To-day's matches are as follows : Hunslet v. Leeds Bramley v. Hull Kingston Hull v. Keighley Wakefield Trinity v. Bradford Broughton Rangers v, Halifax Huddersfield v. Dewsbury Batley v. Rochdale Hornets Runcorn v. York Warrington v. Salford Swinton v. Merthyr Tydvil Treherbert v. Oldham Barrow v. St. Helens Wigan v. Leigh Ebbw Vale v. Widnes

Warrington's home engagement with Salford will be a source o! attra3tion at Wilderspool, for few teams have clone so well here as the Reds. As a matter of fact they have only lost twice at Warrington since 1900, or three times including that year. This is a remarkable record, and one which I do not think any other team can approach. Lest some of my readers doubt the fact, I append the record for the past nine seasons (home matches only). WARRINGTON. SALFORD. G. T. P. G. T. P. 1900 1901 1902 1903 0 1 3 2 5 19 1904 1 1 5 0 0 0 1905 1900 1907 ......... 1908

With anything like consistent form, Warrington should win every League match this month and top the League once more. A 1:1g effort is required to-day. Warrington Too Good for Widnes.

Keen interest was shosa in the meeting of these old West Lancashire rivals at Widnes on Saturday, when over 4,C30 sp; ctstors assembled of whom 1,500 had followed the Warrington men. Both sides were well represented; and a fine game sas witnessed under ideal conditionr The teams were Widnes—Back, Lambert; three guar, ,ers, Ince,Taylor, Reid end Trirton •, halves, Lally and Noon ; forwards, G. Aspey, Rigby, Curt is, Evans, Lloyd and O'Gara Warrington—Back, Mclntyre; three -quarteu, Fish, Dickenson, Taylor and Bradshaw ; halves, Brooks and Jenkins; forwards, O'Mpiley, Neill, Thomas, Shugars, Harmer and Boardman. Referee, Mr. Priestley (Broughton). G. Mpey started end one of the first incidents UO9 a knee injury to O'Neill which kept the international out of the game for several minutes. Widnes attacked in determined fashion, but the Warrington forwards were the stronger set end cleared the invaders out. Brooks mide a strong ion end .passed to Taylor who ,tried to do too much before passing and was held near the home line. The home lot got off-side in trying to rush the ball away and Mclntyre kicked a grind goal for Warrington from near the half-way line. Brook*, Dickenson, Taylor and Bradshaw passed well, but H. Taylor brought the latter down after be had beaten Lambert. A moment later Neill initiated a movement which raw Fish score a brilliant try after Dickenson and Taylor had handled the leather. Mclntyre failed from a difficult position. The " Wirepullets " main- tained the pressure on resuming, but Lambert was obstructed end the free-kick brought relief. Widnes were unlucky a few minutes later, Boardman charging down Turton's kick and running over. Mclntyre failed badly with the shot at gpal. Widnes took up the struggle aga'n keenly, but could not get the bell

from the pack. A bad knock-on by 'Mclntyre placed Warrington in an awkward position, which was cleared by a tremendous forward rush. Widnes had a look in next., for Harry Taylor dodged grandly, and gained ranch ground. Lally augmented and passed to June, Mclntyre being tackled when field- ing the leather. A moment later Taylor was Filed down a foot short—hard luck for the "Chemicals." O'Malley was applauded for a gallant attempt to tackle Lambert, and then Reid got in one of his characteristic strrng runs. Mclntyre lowered the big centre and Warrington were soon back, but Taylor and Dickenson succumbed to numbers. Once more tbe "Chemicals " made the running, and a bot scramble looked threaten- ing for Warrington. A change came over the scene sudden,ly. Brilliant passing between Brooks, Dicken- son, Taylor and Bradshaw ended in Taylor running in finely from centre behind the posts, Thomas goaling. Half-time Warrington 2 goals 3 trics (13 points), Widnes nil.

Widnes re-opened with a strong dribble, but War- ringtor.'s superior tactics put them on the attack again. Dickenson dropped at goal when he should have prayed the ball, and the ball went over the bar, but a free kick was granted to Widnes instantly. Reid put in a strong dash for the Chemicals," but was pulled down heavily, The margins Cetween the teams seemed to take much of the heart out of Widnes, and, indeed, the pace told on both teams. Brooks was too well watched to get his backs going except at irregular intervals. Reid made one or tr. 4 o strorg dashes for the locals, but Widnes hardly looked like scaring. A sensational piece of play was next seen. Fish came into possession near the Warrington quarter flag and set off along the touch line in his old, sty le. Half-a-dozen men challenged him, but he eluded them all, end raced in from centre. The goal shot failed. After this the ball travelled from end to end very rapidly and Widnes made strong Put spasmodic rushes. Harry Taylor initiated the only really dangerous movement end that came to nought. Just on time Warrington pressed hard and were very properly awarded a try for a bad case of obstruction of Bra Ishaw, who was a certain scorer after ranning very speedily. There was no goal, and the final soon sounded with the score :—Warrington 2 goals, 5 tries 19 points; Widnes nil. RUNCORN OUT-PLAY THE HORNETS.

ST. HELENS' BIG WIN.

Played at St. Helens before 6,000 spectators. The visitors arrived without Walker, their full back, and Ind perforce to take the field with twelve players. Teams :77

St. llelens—H. S. Truth', back; J. Flanagan, J. Greenwood, H. Greenwood, and C. Cr c 3vey, three- quarter backs; F. Trenwith and A. F. Kelly. half backs; Bradley, Brilds, See, Prescott. Walker, and Helsby, for- wardr

Bramley—Hope, back; Kirby, Oldershaw, Orton, and Atkins, t'Aree-ouarter hacks; Smith and Hey. balf backs • Stead, Clarke, Harrison, Burnley, and Sharp, forwards. _ _

Referee, Mr. A. Smith, Halifax. Touch judges, Mr. W. Gregory, Runcorn, and Mr. R. Jones, Widnca.l Bramley started the game and at once got dangerous Trialll Laving to kick dear from Oldershaw. St. Helens pressed near the Bramley end, and Brild scored an unimproved try. After a span of even midfield play Trenwith and Kelly combined, the latter hrading on to Creavey, who touched down at the corner. Creevey failed;to augment, Hay, the Bramley half, tricked Kiang, and beating the opposition scored at the corner. Hope failed to improve. Following this Flanagan doubled in, and feling to find an opening transferred to Creerey, who crossed at the corner. Creevey again failed to convert. Half-time score :—St, Helens 3 tries (a points), Bramley 1 1., y(3 points). • Changing ends, St. Helens were penalised for an informality in their own half, and Hope easily kicked a goal. Afte- Flanagan failed to cross from Greenwcod's pars, the same player rr ade amends a moment later in crossing. Drain tailed. Result :—St Helens 3 goals and 7 tries (27 points), Bramley 1 goal and 1 try (5 points).

MATCHES ON MONDAY. Warrington's First Defeat

Fresh from their success at Widnes the Warrington men journeyed to Huddersfield full of confidence ;n their ability to maintain an unbeaten certificate. It was recognised that the opposition was furnished by the most improved team in Yorkshire but with a full team the Wirepullors bld no fears. A; events turned out they met their masters at Fartown. There was a fine day for the match, rid with the Huddersfield holidays being on the crowd rolled up l2'h COO strong. 'bough short of Wagstaffe and Walsh the homesters put a i strong team n the field and were every bit as corfident as the visitors. Mr. Mc Cutcbeon was the riferee, and the teams tine,l up as follows : Warr'ngton,—Mclntyre, back; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor and Biadsbaw, threequarter backs • Brooks nod Jenkirs, half-backs; O'Malley, Thomas, Shugars, Boardman, and Harmer, forwards. Huddersfield—Whitwam, back; A. Rosefeldt, E. Wrigley, J. Bartholomew, and W. Kitchen, threequarter backs; P. Holroyd and J. Davies, half backs ; W. Ainley, W. Brook. D. Clark, W. Trevarthen, F. Charles- worth and J. Shallcrose, forwards. Thomas started the game in conditions which did not favour either side, and- a good return was made by Bartholomew who ran to centre before giving a pass which went in touch. Warrington responded with a forward rush beaded by O'Malley and Thomas, the movement being checked in fine style by Wrigley who punted powerfully. Warrington were persistent and again forced their way to the home qnarter where Thomas picked no and threw wide to Bradshaw, the right-winger failing to gather. Whitwam failed to clear a punt, and Davies played the I all off-side in front of his own goal The penalty was promptly awarded and Thomas just grazed the left upright with ' a shot at goal. This narrow escar ) roused the home team and they quickly drove Warrington back by strong forward play and smatt handling. Mclntyre was tested and had his kick charged near the line, Fish sending in touch in the nick of time. A fine combined movement by Brooks, Dickenson, Taylor and Bradshaw took play past centre again but the attack was of short duration. Huddersfield were having the better of the game and Wrigley took a pnalty in good posi- tion but was yards wide. Some hot work in the Warrington quarter followed, Dickenson twice marking in critical situations. Wrigley had another pop at goal —this time from half-way—and was only a little wide of the object. Quick passing by Huddersfield saw Kitchen with fin opening on the lett, but he knocked on when there was only the frill back to beat. A smart run by Fish changed the venue and it looked as if there would be no score before half-time. Five minutes from the interval however. Dickenson was bowled over after punting, and the bail coming loose the -Huddersfield backs brndled well. Rosenfeld passed inside to Ills foraards and Brooks raced over under the posts, le Holroyd kfcng an easy goal. Half-time: Hudders- field 5 points, Waisington, nil Warrington resumed in aggrersive fashior the home team being without Holroyd for a quarter of an hour. Brooks made a strong run but held to long and was tackled. Wrigley made a great effort at centre and forced his way through. lee ball was quickly sent r Iross to the wing and Bradshaw fiy-kicled straight into Kitchen's hands. lite wing man gathered well and running with great determination got over at the corner. Wrigley kicked a flue goal from touch and the crowd cheered itself hoarse. Warrington were not dbne with, however, and Fish led the way to the other end with a long inn down touch. Dickenson took a pass from the act urn and dropped at goel when a pass would have answered better. The hone lot were overplayed for a time, and a well-judged crosskick by Mcletyre found touch near the line. Wrigley broke away agair but a-ae downed by Fish. The Warrington crack then r ;riled cheers by a sterling effort which covered half the length of the field and only failed a ' few yards from the line where numbeis prevailee A moment later Fish made another effort but was grassed on the 25 line. There was a lively struggle here and Shagais and Brook (Huddersfield) received rna-ching lorders as the result of a blow or two. Fich was promin- ent again before the -close and kicked a penalty goal near time. Final: Huddersfield, 2 goalr, - tries (10 points) ; Warringtlr, 1 goal (2 points).

WARRINGTON "A " V. WIDNES "A."

Special interest centred in this match at Wilderspcol on Satarday, chiefly owing to the appearance of Frawley, the Australian threequarter, who, though not yet completely recovered from the injury to his ankle, had to play in this match to qualify for the Lancashire Cup tie. Smith, a new player from gtockton Heath, alio figured in the threequar ter line,and gave a smart display. Widnes were well represented and started promisingly. Gibbs effects two smart saves and eventually the Warrington forwards, led by Chesters and Beltou,raced to the Widnes end, where Smith made two good efforts to break through. A parsing movement, initiated by Cartwright, ended in Cheaters transferring on to Brad- shaw, the goal shot proving futile Warrington still pressed and only a taw minutes afterwards Smith accepted a pass from Lloyd and scored an excellent try. Daintith added the goal. Lloyd was again prominent in a strert break-away and Cartwright made an opening for Smith, but the pass was not accepted. Widnes, by good passing. worked their way to the home quarters, but he tackling was keen and the visitors ware once more forced to their own territory. Frawley though handicapped by his injury, was on the aleit and did not allow the opposition any opportueities to make head- way. At half-time Warrington led by eight points t nil. Frawley now retired from the field and was warmly cheered. Turning round the homesters imn ediatele forced the pace, and only a few minutes had elapsed before C:rtwright put on the floishing touch to a dis- play of cross dribbling. P: Boardman, who had taken Frawley's place in the three-quarter line, made a capable substitute, and twice was pulled up only inches from the line. Jordan augmented the home score, after the forwards had done splendid work, whilst following a smart movement by Lloyd, Widnes scored a fine try sfter good p‘assing, Bradshaw led Warr:ngton on the attack near the close and Daintith scored. NORTHERN SCHOOLS LEAGUE.

WIDNES SCHOOLBOYS V. WAR EIINGTON SCHOOLBOY,

This match was played at 'Wilderspool en Saturday before the" A" team match. In the first hslf Widnes, who were by far the heavier set, were soon on the aggressive. WARRINGTON LBIONS v. ST. HELENS TOWN. The Albions entertained St. llelens Town at Liver- pool-road on Saturday afternoon in a friendly encounter before a moderate attendance. l no teams were as follows :—Albions : Trow ; Sherf, Sergeant ; B. Whitfield, Dickinson, Tweedale ; Ball, Whitfield, Thomas, Hunter, and Cheaters. St.llelens H.Swinton, Yates, ,?eters ; Moris, E. Swinton, 11 Stott ; Barton, Bluff, Layford, Hall and Birchall. It was evident from the start that St. Helens were a much better team and soon after the kick-off play was herd in the home team's quarter and Birchall had a shot which went wide or the mark. Soon after the ball was in play the Albions rushed down the field and Chesterc had a tag for goal which just missed, the ball bitting the netting. More tapping of the ball and then Hunter seizing a chance ran down and had a clean shot at goal which was intercepted by Yates who pushed the ball into touch. Play again centered round the St. Helens goal and Hunter tried to get the bull throush with a sraatt header, bat Swinton cleared. Every minute or two a man of either side had a ;,zy for goal but none were succesaful until Hail with.a resh defeated Trow. This was added to a moment later by a goal for St. Helens by Birchall. The Albions claimed a penalty but Swinton succeeded in saving the ball from a kick by Hunter. Half-time: St. Helens two, Albions nil. Resuming the garce, St. Helens again pressed, but the ball was punched iato the centre, and there hostilities were waged for a little while. A little combination by thil Albions and Chesters was afforded a good chance, but his shot was Wt ak and wide. Barton had a shot from the corner, but it just missed Cheaters again had an opening but ho failed. Barton had a *-y for goal from the corner which Trow stopped, but knocked the ball in the netting, therefare giving another goal for St. Helens. Hunter secured the ball

for himself, dribbled and bad a shot at goal, which found its mark, thus notching the Albions first goal. Langford also found the netting. Final result :—St. Helens Town 6, St. Albions 1.

FOOTBALL GOSSIP.

BY B. BENNISON.

ILLUSTRATED BY JACK DODWORTII

The first month of a new football season llt;‘, ing closed, it is interesting—one cannot say in. structive, for happenings up to date may lead t( all sorts of misconceptions—to see exactly whai our big clubs have done. One is much surprisce at the inability of Sheffield Wednesday to dc anything right; *Preston North End seem to sug gest, as they did last season, that they need for wards; the strength is ail behind. Bury have done no better than expected. Chelsea do tot play in a convincing way at all; good forwards should not find it very difficult to cat an easy

way through their defence. Middlesbrough, aftet an uncommonly good beginning., have taken tc wobbling; Woolwich Arsenal are just moderate, no better and no worse than I thought they would be. Middlesbrough's Decline.

Notts Forest have advanced in a surprising, almost wonderful, way; had it not been for theit very heavy fall against Liverpool, they woulc have had an amazing record for the first month. Then we have Bradford, after their sorry show last season, playing the part of wonder-workers; Aston Villa carry themselves like a side witl: such high traditions should do; they have been really brilliant on occasion, but . somehow I fancy there will be many days when they will lute their "form" completely. Desertion of "form" is one of the many curious features of the play of our leading clubs. Take Sheffield Wednesday as an example. There does not ap- pear any reason, knowing as I do the nlen who are quartered at Owlerton, why they should have played half a dozen -matches without once taking the maximum number of points;, neither "an ,there be any explanation why Middles- broug,h, after giving an exhilarating exhibition of how to get goals, should become so ineffective as they have been in at least two of their most recent games. Lhave never been able to under- stand why l‘liddlesbrough each season should at some period or othr prejudice their chances of remaining in their .present company, for the men they have have nearly always been most able exponents of the game. The re are, kr in- stance, tremendous possibilities in the forward rank, and though there are one or two -old stagers: in defence, the team, on paper at least, could not be bettered. Tottenham Hotspur, like Notts County and Woolwich Arsenal, scored their first victory a week ago. Chelsea, whom Woolwich Arsenal beat, are very much inclined to go to pieces when they are engaged in a match with the players at. Plumstead; and I was not at all sur- prised that the West Londen club faded. Frankly, I have not much confidence in Chelsea. If Tottenham be wise, they will not coddle them- selves into believing that the three-goal victory over Sheffield Wednesday means that their -team came up to First League requirements. The "Spurs" are not very strong in de-fence; and they will be fortunate if they escape a great deal of trouble. Recruits are wanted at Tottenham. Bert Freema..'s Usefulness.

When Newcastle opened the season with a little tour they contrived to make Everton look very small indeed; Newcastle just won any- how, and there was no mistaking the fact that they gave a very brilliant exposition at Goodi- son Park. 011 Q feared that Everton were in for a rather lean season, but here we have them

avenging their early defeat, and Newcastle, flushed with the honours of their success at Sun- derland the previous week, were hit very hard indeed. The usefulness of Bert Freeman is un- questionable. I am sure that Everton, when they') secured him from WooZwich Arsenal, never expected that he would develop into such a treasure. Freeman is a very lucky young man to have two such men helping him. as Young and Coleman. With all due respect to Freeman. I am sure he will confeFii that he owes much lo them.. You could not wish for two better inside men than Young and Coleman, whose de- Parture from Plumstead caused so much di,;- appointment. Change of quarters has made Freeman one of the most dangerous centre-for- wards we have; his dash through the backs is a fearsome thing ;. it must be seen to be believed. It will be interesting to see how Freeman's brother Walter goes on at Birmingham. Walter is not such a fearless player as Bert, .but the latter has much talent. I am rather sur- prised that Fulham allowed him to go to the Birmingham club. Fulham have quite a little army of -players, Wit considering how dis- appointing the forwards have bc-en so far, they do not seem to have go: the right men to ivin a place in the First Division.

Manchester City's New Goalkeeper. Wet Bromwich Albion had a high old time at Craven Cottage. It will perhaps be remem- bered that they were pretty deci,iively beaten when they were last on the ground of the Lon- don club. They made no mistake this time, and took their revenge with keen ttlish. Early im-

:EEFI7,

pressions very often lead one asiray, and I have an idea that the present Wert Bromwich team is very much superior to that which did duty last season. If the Throstles do not get baek to the -First League this time I shall be very much surprised. -Blackpool continue to do very well indeed; it was good business to play a draw with _.Matichester City, whose officials, by the way, have been on the prowl for new players. They have bagged a- most accomplished goalkeeper in Lyall, who for so long did _duty for •, Sheffield Wednesday.. The transfer mast have been a very high one.. Sunderland. I am told, would have been very glad of Lyall. though with Roe and well there could not have been much scope for the Scottish Inter- national. Since the day of old Jack Hillman Manchester City have spent a lot of money on goalkeTers. If my memory Oe rre s me rightly, they paid £I,COO for Smith, of Leicester Fosse.

We have seen tha liehercians, and on Melt' showing in their first match at Ipswich, with a team got togethei by the Arnat2Lir Football Association, they are no gr.:-at shakes. As a taatter of -fact. -rhos have much to learn.

THE EXAMINER;

SATURDAY, OCTOBER 22ND, 1909.

The first note of the WARMING TO coming conflict was THEs'FRAY. struck in the. House of Lords on Wednesday. Lord LANGFORD, desirous of supporting the second reading of the Irish Bill, wished to know whether it would be possible to amend the financial proposals of the Bill in Com- inittee. The reply of Lord CREWE was yery Inuch to the point. He said that to increase 'as to vary or diminish, a charge on public funds has not ,hitherto been considered to fall Vithin the province of the House of Lords. 1' hat seems to us to be a statement °rale Nthole case. The Lords have nothing to do 'With tne financial arrangements of the 'country. This is not a consequence of a declaration made in the Seventeenth Century, as We find so many of our contemporaries contending. It gees back to EDWARDIAN claYS—over at least doubly as large a space 43f time as that which is claimed for the first assertion by the House of Commons of its absolute control over finance. • The actual situation, and the constitutonal llosition are very well understood by the

leading men in both Ho-uses- of Parliament. We are quite convinced that neither Mr. BALFOUR nor Lord LANSDOWNE will recom- mend either the rejection or the hanging up of the Budget. If we were to use -a common colloquial expressiono,ve should say that they are not such fools. So far the Peers are subject to restraint; they will be required to behave reasonably. They were permitted to tear the Housing and Town Planning Bill to pieces. They are to be permitted to use their teeth on the Irish Land Bill. What does this mean? It means that they are to treat the Budget Bill as if they were persons with satiated appetites. They will have had a free run of the arena before the Finance Bill goes before them, and by that thpe they will be devouring monsters no more. This, at any rate, is the official Tory arrangement. The leaders of the party will not permit their followers to destroy the Budget if they can prevent such a catastrophe. But they neither know what they can do nor what they can prevent.

We may be wrong, of course. We speak only from conviction and from experience; but we are quite strongly convinced that the Lords will not take the risk of treating the Finance Bill as if it were a measure dealing, let us say, with sewage or the making of a country road. If they had intended to go to extremes, they would have let other Govern- ment measures slip through, as they did in the case of the Trades Disputes Bill. When they wreck minor measures, they mean to signify that they have no intention of reject- ing those which are of supreme importance. • This is what we take to be the present posi- tion of affairs as regards the intentions of the leaders of the Tory party. But the leaders of the Tory Party do not know who are their followers in the House of Lords. The MARQUIS OF LANSDOWNE can command those members of his party who 'put in any- thing like a regular attendance. He has no control over the much larger number of noble lords who only put in an appearance when they believe that their personal interests are in danger. So that it comes to this, that the interests of the _ country and the character of the Constitution are to be placed at the mercy of two or three hundred members of the House of Lords who do not think the interests of the country to be of sufficient impprtance to justify them on ordinary occasions in paying a cab-fare from Hyde Park Corner to Westminster.

Thursday's "Ma n - A LEAD FOR chester Guardian " conJ

CHURCHMEN. tamed a trenchant article written under the nom de plume of " Quartus " by so well- known and respected a Church dignitary as Canon Hicks. The subject is one that con- cerns every Churchman, cleric and lay. At the present moment many honest,enlightened, but thoroughgoing Churchmen are hesitating about their course of action (i.e. how to vote at the next General Election). They have been told that the Budget is Socialism by a no less responsible authority than Mr. Balfour, and they have heard it said oft and again that Socialism is but another name for spoliation, and the negation of Christianity, and the destruction of the marriage tie. If they have not read and thought deeply they may believe these things. Canon Hicks goes to considerable trouble to meet the difficulties of such gentlemen .and to point the way clearly for them. They need not boggle at the bogey of" Socialism "—" which is being dressed.up with fresh terrors and exaggera- tions by the ehemies of democracy," to quote the canon's truthful and reassuring words. "At Oxford and Cambridge the abler and more thoughtful minds are going over in crowds to Socialistic principles." . 1 " Within the Church of England, and outside of it, the younger and earnest men are eager for social reform and impatient to see it achieved' • • • • "The Church is quickening her pace and not too soon. If she fears revolu- tion, let her take sides with the democracy, there is no safety elsewhere." These are weighty words, inviting the grave consider- ation of all Churclimen who feel themselves hide-bound by prejudice and patronage. If they are true to themselves and their sacred mission they will let conscience decide. There is no middle course. Churchmen must either "follow the gleam" of progress, or stake the prosperity and privileges of the Church upon the party of reaction.

The yearly business MESSRS. PEARSON meeting of the Pearson AND KNOWLES and Knowles Coal and ANNUAL MEETING. Iron Co., Ltd., has always a special inter- est for Warrington-.—largely because the firm is the biggest employer of labour in the town, and partly because its operations give a reli- able indication of the state of the coal and iron trades. We are glad to be -able to congratu- late Messrs. Pearson and Knowles on being in a position to present another sound balance sheet to their shareholders at the 36th annual meeting last Monday. The dividend is less than it was twelve months ago, but the results of the year's work- ing cannot be looked upon as other than satisfactory considering the depression which has existed in the iron and coal industries. A profit of £61,890 is a substantial earning in bad times. Mr. Harmood Banner, M.P., the chairman of the Company, as usual had some interesting observations to make in his annual review. We note that he patted the Company on the back because it had paid out £620,956 in wages and only £40,220 in dividend to shareholders=a distribution between labour and capital of "the nett proceeds of manufacture" which he thought combated the oft-heard contention that labour did not get its fair share. We really wonder how such an expert in figures as Mr. Banner can imagine that the illustra- tion is convincing, because even the confid- ing general public will scarcely believe that he and his fellow shareholders are in busi- ness for their health. As a matter of fact the figures themselves will not bear analysis. The dividend may, it is true, only absorb £40,220 but in addition £20,000 has been added to the reserve fund awl a considerable sum taken. out of profits for extensions and renewals. In another year the whole of the profit might be absorbed by the erection of new works—if the directors so willed— and Mr. Banner could claim with the same small amount of reason that labour had got all, and capital nothing! Such a comparison as he offers for public consumption° is hardly _worthy of the esteemed chairman of the company. We hope Messrs. Pearson and Knowles will con- tinue to prosper, but we must not be gulled into thinking that the be-all and end-all of

- IS° .. ~ --' - # ;II ' t 1 4 •, ,` ;Ait. . A ` , .I. ri ;-4:', ''' j e, ,/ 1 , 1 . . 1 1, I ' .',4-... ,; ::.t ,:• 'I , :'J 4 ' l'i, 4.. ' ''.' • 0 , . • f ii ... f; ; • i„ - 1_,.. ,__ ..... _.....\ ,

their vast operations is the earning of £620,956 in order to find employment for several thousand workers. The men do a little honest earning also, and if Mr. Banner wishes to re-adjust matters between capital and labour he must be prepared to meet labour's contention that some share in the profits, over and above bare wage, is its just due.

With every promise of THE DEBATING a successful session, the SOCIETY. Warrington Parlia- mentary Debating Society resumed its consideration of affairs of State on Tuesday evening. It was pleasing to note the enthusiasm and good feeling which pervaded the assembly and the Society is•unquestionably doing a useful and com- mendable work in enabling budding politi- cians of the various schools to meet for keen yet friendly debates on the great topics of the day. The Speaker aptly expressed the hope, which was generally endorsed, that none of the bitterness which a general election may engender will find its way into the pre- cincts of the House. The Government pro- gramme as outlined by the Premier (Mr.W.H. Stansfield) is a somewhat ambitious one, and we fear some of the leaders of Conservatism will be inclined to regard it as quite revolutionary. The possibility of the closing of public houses on election days being embodied in a Tory measure seems too good to be true. It does not,, however, square with the attitude of the party which has so consistently endeavoured to thwart temper- ance reform. There is much in the Premier's forecast which will probably command the support of all sections of the House, but from the debating standpoint ' a more frankly Conservative and controversial programme would have been preferable. There will soon be more difficulty in defining Conservatism than Socialism.

Yet another scare of ANOTHER "RUINED "ruined industries." INDUSTRY." industriously _circu-

lated in the Protec- tionist Press, has been exposed and made ridiculous. And the instrument of this exposure is actually a Unionist Peer, who on general grounds is opposed to the Budget. Earl De La Warr, who from his own experi- ence in devolving his property can speak with knowledge about the state of the market for land, has written to the Press complaining of the fiction invented by Protectionist organs ,that the Budget has depreciated the value of land. On the con- trary, he asserts with conviction that the value of land is distinctly and in some cases enormously increasing, and any fall that may have taken place in house property set in two years before the Budget. And it is on the strength of reckless mis-statement such as this pretended slump in the property market that we are asked to regard the Budget as so " revolutionary " that it can only be met by a revolution in constitu- stitution'al principles firmly, established for three hundred years!

Mr. Lloyd-George has LOCAL AUTHORITIES very naturally and AND THE LAND -very wisely decided to TAXES. hold over for consulta- tion with the local authorities the allocation of the moiety of the land taxes which is assigned to them. As he pointed out on Monday, this decision will involve only a very slight delay in allocation, after the proceeds have accrued. Mr. Balfour, in trying to make capital out of this, exposed himself to a strong counter-attack from Mr. Lloyd- George, who showed up the absurdity of the Opposition's attitude in calling the half of the land taxes which goes to the National Exchequer robbery. It is true that Mr. Balfour had hedged his own case round with some flimsy qualifications. But a prominent Tariff Reformer like Mr. F. E. Smith is now attacking the Budget for its land taxes and yet hesitating to go back on the support which he gave in his election address to taxation of increment • value by local authorities.

The latest manceuvre THE ELECTION of the Opposition •is to ISSUE. pretend that the dis-

solution which the House of Lords threatens to bring about is to be fought on the Budget alone. There is even an attempt to make out that the House of Lords are taking quite a democratic step. "After all," they say, "what are they doing but asking for a popular mandate. Even if they are beaten, they would have done quite a useful thing. They will have allowed the people to decide!" Now, just see where that leads. It means that the people are to be forced to have a General Election on every Budget to which the Lords object. If that view is accepted, then we may have another General Election next year on the same issue. The country may be kept in a perpetual state of chaos. It will be impossible for any Liberal Government to levy its taxes. That view is clearly intolerable. The great point to remember is that if the Lords compel a dis- solution this year on the Budget, it must be the last dissolution of the kind. The ques- tion to be decided at the coming General Election is not only the "Budget," but the "Lords."

Death of a Crimean Veteran.

Andiber of the rapidly diminishing band of Crimean and Indian Mutiny veterans passed away on .Sunday, in the person of Sergeant William Bloom- field, 63a, Winwick-street. The deceased, who would have been 81 years of age on Thursday, had a fine record of acti:e service to his credit. He enlisted in the 95th Regiment on February Bth, 1846, and after several years' service in China, he sailed for the Crimea in 1854. He took part in the battles ,of Alma and • Inkerman and in the siege and capture of Sebastopol. The Crimean campaign over, he returned home but in 1857 his regiment\ was drafted to India where the mutiny had broken out,thad Sergt. Bloomfield served through the long struggle for British supremacy. He retired from active service on August 26th, 1867, afterwards joining the Militia staff for nine years. For 23 years the deceased held the position of caretaker of the Fever Hospital, lie was a staunch Conservative and took a keen interest in politics. He leaves a widow, four sons, and a daughter. Two of the sons are serving in the regiment with . which their father saw so much service, while another is bandmaster at; the Derby Depot. The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Thursday afternoon with full military honours. The cortege left the house of the deceased's son,illaydock-street, headed by the Orford Barracks band, 21 of the firing party, and about 100 of thf rank and file. The Rev. James Davies, vicar of St. Ann's, performed the last rites. Whilst at the graveside 3 volleys were fired and the band played the" Dead March" in Saul with impressive effeet. , The buglers also sounded the "Last Post." Amongst those present were, Colour- Sergeants Cummins and Bowers (Indian veterans) Alderman Smethurst (representing the local Veterans Fund) Mr. W. T Flood(representing the Health Depart- ment) Alderman Bennett and Mr. NV. Holbrook (Con- servative Association). Amongst the many wreaths received was one from the officers and men of the Orford Barracks.
