PrES AND NOTIONS.

Colonel Seely M.P., has promised to address a Parr Hall meeting under the auspices of the Liberal Association on November 18th. * * *

Mr. J. Howes, junr., the Liberal Registra- tion agent for Warrington is to be married on October 23rd. We consider this an excel- lent preliminary for him to the general election. There is nothing like having a hearth and home to fight for. * * *

Mr. Harwood Banner, M.P. and Mr. J. J. Bleckly both see signs of improvement in the iron trade, and even hold hopes of the coal trade. In view of the set-back to Tariff Reform, the advance of the Budget, and the spread of " Socialism," optimism from such quarters is most reassuring. * *

Mr. G. C. Rees, the Liverpool barrister,who took .a prominent part in the defence in the Warrington election petition proceedings, has just opened his winter political campaign at Bangor, in the constituency of the Chancellor of the Exchequer. During October he will also address meetings at Manchester, Caergwrle, Nelson, Dukinfield and Liverpool.

The Chinese are now nearly all gone from the Rand. On August 31st the number had been reduced to 5,361, and of these 2,164 left a week ago, bringing the total down to just over 3,000. Of these, 1,300 leave on or about December 9th. All the Chinese who were on the Rand when the Liberal Government took office have left; but it will be remembered that 13,000 permits for licences had been granted which could not be cancelled. But for these permits the Chinese would by this time have all been repatriated. - * * *

The Conservative van in charge of Mr. Paton, of Oldham, has had rather a stormy passage this week. Last Saturday the Socialists would hardly give the speaker a hearing, and nearly everywhere he has met with opposition and criticism. At Latchford on Thursday evening there were numerous lively passages and the meeting closed with "three cheers for the Budget." The "oppo- sition" is not organi,sed, but spontaneous. Commencing on October 11th a Liberal van will tour the constituency in charge of Mr. Lewis and Councillor J. Kendall. They should find the soil well prepared for their special Budget seed.

The month of October is 'likely to be the most critical time in the history of England since 1832. The House of Commons have now finished the Committee stage of the Budget Bill, and the remaining stages will be disposed of within the next fortnight. The bill will reach the House of Lcrrds about the middle of the month. What will happen then? The Lords may slacken or quicken the-crisis, but before the end of the present month they must come to a decision. It is probable that before October 30th they will have decided whether to pass or reject the Budget Bill. * * *

There remains, therefore, a month for them to make their decision, and it is to be hoped that during that time the wild counsels at present in favour may have become a little tamer. But if we may judge from the present ouitopk, it would adipear almost certain that they are going to reject the Bill. How has that situation come about? Be it noted that the responsible Conservative leaders have never advocated rejection. Lord Lansdowne has been known to be against it throughout the summer. Lord Rothschild is said to be against it on financial grounds. Lord St. Alwyn is also reputed. to be opposed to tejection, together with most of the other responsible heads of the old Tory Party. They realise that to throw out the Budget means to begin a revolution which may not end in the way they desire. * * *

No, it is not the old Conservatives who are precipitating this crisis. It is the Tariff Reformers on the one hand and the brewers on the other. Lord Rosebery hesitated to take so momentous a step, but Mr. Joseph Chamberlain did not hesitate at all. Writing to the Bingley Hall meeting for the Tariff Reformers, he urged the House of Lords to throw out the bill. But the strongest force of all is the brewers. They are not being asked to pay more than a fair share of the increased value of the monopoly which they possess; but encouraged by their triumph of last, year, what is called 'the - Trade" have from the very beginning shown the greatest confidence in their power to upset the Budget. Led by the London brewers, they have refused to accept Mr. Lloyd-George's most moderate overtures. At a critical moment in the history Qf the Budget they refused to supply the Treasury with the material for revising the scale of on-licence duties. They thus lost all chance of com- promise ; for it was impossible for the Treasury to make any revision without material which could only bo provided for them by the " Trade" itself. They thus staked everything on a fight. * *

If rumour speaks Correctly, the brewers have during the past fortnight carried this attitude a step further. They have told the Conservatives quite plainly that unless the Lords throw out the Budget they will with- draw their contributions to the Conservative funds. That is a threat which reminds us rather ominously of some of the worst inci- dents in American politics, and it is sincerely to be hoped that if the threat has been made the Conservative Party is still true enough, to British traditions of political purity to treat it with scorn. If the " Trade " have been speculating in a "revolution," they must take the result on their own shoulders. * * *

Meanwhile, as the campaign against the Budget goes on in tile country it becomes, in the words which Alice used in "Wonder- land," " curiouser and curiouser." For instance, there is a decided rift in the lute on the subject of the land clauses. The party of the Dukes wants to throw out the Budget because of its land clauses ; but in the meantime the Tariff Reformers want to adopt the land clauses for their own purposes. The only difference between these politicians and the Government is that the Tariff Reformers want to keep the revenue for the rates alone, and the Government want to divide it between the rates and the taxes. Really the talk about the Budget being a " revolution " becomes rather thin! • * *

Even the best of our public speakers have trouble with their hands while they are talking. Their difficulty expresses itself in curious mannerisms. Mr. Lloyd-George has a habit of extending his arms and twitching his cuffs into notice; Mr. Balfour holds loosely to the lapels of his coat, and never lets go; Lord Hugh Cecil and Mr. Birrell resemble one another in this, if in little else, that they are always nervously engaged in rubbing their hands or bending back their fingers. And so on. Mr. F. E. Smith appar- ently gets over the difficulty, when he ii conscious of it, by thrusting his hands into his pockets. When he rose to address the

meeting at Belmont Hall on Saturday aft-er- noon (says the "Liverpool Post and Mercury") he assumed this attitude, and a member of his audience promptly got up and solemnly suggested that the speaker should -take his hands out of his pockets. As the meeting was composed largely of members of his own executive, it is to be hoped that Mr. Smith will not be so unkind as to suggest that the interruption was organised, fomented, or even countenanced by the Liberal Press. * * *

• Mr. liArlter Long, was fairly cornered in the course of his speech on Wednesday night at Cardiff: Mr. Long went on to say that it was incredible, and he did not think intelligent •men really believed at this time of day that -anyone would wish to tax the people's food. No, one held that view. (A Voice: "Joey does.") " Evidently," retorted Mr. Long, "that gentleman has not taken the trouble to read the right hon. gentleman's speeches." But, surely, this is precisely what "that gentleman" had done. For (to give only only one quotation out of dozens that might be given) Mr. Chamberlain said, in the House of Commons, on May 28th, 1903:

If you are to give-a preference to the Colonies . . . you must put a tax on food. This is explicit enough in all coescience, and we do not understand how Mr. Long could so heatedly deny an absolute fact, even if unpalatable.

Edward Hopley, late of 41, Buttenuarket-street, dealer in musical Instruments appeared for his public examination at the Warrington Bankruptcy Court yesterday. The' liabilities amounted to £BO and there were no assets. He took the business over from Mr. Smith, of Widnes, in April 1908 and he was examined principally as to whether the business was really his or whether he was not managing it for Mr. Smith. He asserted that the business was his and the examinition was closed.

Reilway Clerk as Foreign Missionary.

On Tuesday afternoon an interestin% presentation was made at the London and North-Western Railway Company's Offices, Goods Department, Bank Quay Station, to Mr. F. Foster (of Weaverham), who is leaving the service of the railway eompany to go out to Canada as a Missionary attached to the Wes!eyan Church. Mr. Foster is 24 years old, and has had ten years service with the railway company. The clerical staff assembled in the office of the goods agent. Mr. Townley made the presentation, and Mr. Foster suit- ably replied. The present consisted of a case of three razors and shwing outfit, writing case and a pocket book. Mr. Foster sails from Liverpool on the 13th inst., bound for Calgarry. TABLE AND HANGING LAMPS, new designs now showing.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt.

Counties' StoOland Bazaar. A great Counties' Storyland Bazaar in aid of the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union has been held in the Free Trade Hall, Manchester, this week and will continue until this (Saturday) evening. The.effort is part of the forward movement of the Band of Hope Union to ;also £20,000 in' 1909 for the temperance training of the child. It is hoped to realize £6,000 by tne bazaar which is on a very large scale, the stalls representing all parts of Lancashire and Cheshire. One stall is provided by St. Helens and Warrington, the Warrington workers who have interested themselves in the effort being :—Tresident, Mrs. A. 11. Crosfield ; acting president, Mrs. J. Dodds; treasurer, Mr. I. Pierce Williams; secretaries, Mr. F. Gandy and Miss Emily lackson ; committee. Mesdames Fearnley, . Shaw Tanner, Whitfield, Misses Olive Bacon, F. Beswick, B. Dale, A. M. Dodds, M. Fletcher, Irwin, A. Oakley, Proffit, M. Quarry, Whalley; Messrs. Cartwright, Dale, Firth, Lew, Powney and Whitfield. The bazaar was opened on Tuesday by the Countess of Warwick; on Wednesday by the Lord and Lady Mayoress of Liverpool, on Thursday by the Hon. Lady Barlow, and on Friday by the Hon. Mrs. Eliot Yorke. To- day it will be -opened by Sir W. P. Hartley. Up to Thursday evening the proceeds amounted to £3,500.

It will be remembered that several years ago Mr. Roberts presented the Warrington Liberal Club with the premises in Bewsey-street, of which he was the owner.

Warwick (28), of 51, Folly-lane, Warrington, whose body was found in the Old Quay Canal on Thursday. The deceased had been employed by the L. and N.-W. Co., as a fireman, but had been in ill-health for some time. He left his home a week ago to get a medical certificate, but had been missing ever since. A Nation in Arms." "A Nation in Arms," has set out on its missionary travels, and ha S arrived at the Queen's Theatre, Manchester. Though twb opinions may still exist as to the wisdom of Mr. Townroe's political objects and the soundness of his strategical theories, there can be no doubt that the play, regarded as an entertainment, if Mr. Townroe will forgive so frivolous a point of view, is all the better for half-a-dozen performances in the public eye and for pruning and polishing to which the author has subjected his last act.— Manchester Guardian."

Sudden Death of a Spiritualist. Mr. John Moorey, one of the most respected mem- bers of the Warrington Spiritualist Society, Awl away suddenly .on Sunday night. While perf his usual duties at the last service, or after-circle at the Spiritualist Hall, he was suddenly seized with what proved to be a paralytic stroke. Dr. Smith was sent for and the police ambulance van, and by direc- tion of the chotor he was conveyed to his home, 20, Dalton-bank, off Manchester-roftd. He never recovered consciousness and death took place about midnight. Mr. Moorey was on honoured vice-president of the Spiritualist Society, but the duty he liked to carry out was that of attendant at the door and collector of the offertory. He delighted in" missionary " work, and was very earnest in his advocacy of Spiritualism. He was about 70 years of age, and has left a family of four sons and three daughters. His wife had pre-deceased him. By trade he was a tailor, and worked for the late Mr. Mawson, and subse- quently for the Grand Clothing Hall Company, but a few months ago gave up business owing to poor health and failing eyesight.

WHAT IS A MILITARY TATTOO?

"Our Local Territorial Battalion and Transport Company are,advertising " A Grand Zfilitary Torch- light Tattoo "in Victoria Park on the 14th inst. We find that in consequence many of the public ara asking the above question Ivhich we will endeavour briefly to answer. In every military camp or barracks throughout the British Empire a Soldier learns the time and to a gteat extent his orders from the bugle calls which may be somewhat aptly described as the soldier's timepiece. The bugle sounds repeatedly throughout a soldier's day from "reveille " to "lights out." At sunset the bugle call is named. " retreat" which we presume applies to His Majesty the sun, and not to a British soldier who hates retreating like poison. At 9.30 p.m. a call named "First Post" is sounded, and at 10 p.m. "Last Post" or " Tattoo '.' ; this is the call which is sounded over a soldier's grave, and is the last tribute of his comrades after laying him in his last lonely bed. After "Last Post " tht; bugle sounds once more, namely at 10.15 p.m., which call is known as "Lights out, when all lights in Barrack rooms or tents must be extinguished. After this no call except the " Alarm " (when necessary) sounds until reveille the following morning. In large camps or garrisons what is called " Tattoo " is beaten on the, drums, and a march played by the passed bugles, drums and fifes in Scotch regiments by the pipers from 9.30 (" First Post ") until 10 p.m. (" Last Post.") During this half-hour the rolls are called in the tents or barrack-rooms by the Orderly-Sergeants on duty who take a list of all absentees (without leave) to the parade ground and hand them to the Sergeant- Major who, in turn hands them to the Orderly Officer there present to be deal with the following morning. It often happens that in large camps and on some great occasion • in a large Garrison on one night this so-called " Tattoo " is made into a great " Function " in which the whole of the brass bands, 'bugles, drums, pipes and fifes from every unit assemble for the "Tattoo" and in addition a large number of, torch-bearers from the troops, .some of ,whom sec •mpany the bands, others performing a number of evolutions by themselves to music played by the massed bands and buglers etc. When our local Territorial Battalion (then the Ist V.B. South Lpnca- shire Regiment) was encamped as a unit of the 30th Field Army Brigade on Salisbury Plain, during two of these camps one of these "Tattoos" took' place in Tidworth Park, then the headquarters of Sir lan Hamilton commanding the district. Lady-Hamilton was kind enough to invite a proportion of the War- rington officers (among others) to view the " Tattoo " from the terrace of Tidworth House as the guests of herself and the - General, while all the other troops had an opportunity of witnessing !it from the Park. The second of these Tattoos are arranged by General Bethune and was followed by an illuminated attack on an armoured train. It is to endeavour to give the public of Warrington an oppor- tunity of witnessing a spectacle on somewhat similar lines that has induced the local Territorials to organise the " Tattoo " on the 14th inst. The Band of the 8 40th Regimental Depot from Orford Barracks, the four bands of the South Lancashire (Territorial) Infantry Brigade, the band of the Army Service Corps (West Lancashire Division), the bugle bands of the 4th and sth Battalions, South Lancaseire Regiment, and the Pipers of the Liverpool Scottish, in all 300 performer's will take part. In addition there will be some 400 or 500 torch bearers from the 4th South Lancashire. The local members of. the Church Lads' Brigade and Boy Scouts to the number of 200 will also assist. The first part will be the "Tattoo " and the second a Bivouac and a night attack upon it ; this will be as far as possible spectacular and must not ire judged too critically as a military sight, which would naturally be mostly an affair of cold steel with probably no firing and as little light as possible. The evening will conclude with a grand illuminated rendering of the National An them. Charges of is. and Gd. will be made for admission to the Park, and any balance after expenses will be divided between local charities and various regimental organisations.

ST. ANN'S HARVEST FESTIVAL.

The harvest festival services in connec ion with St. Ann's Church were commenced on Thursday evening, the Rev. J. R. Jones, Vicar of Great Sankey, being the preacher. He took for his text "He was known to them at the breaking of bread."—Having explained the incident in connection with the text, he theaapplied it to every day practice, emphasising how to make oneself known by good deeds in ordinary life. During the service the choir, under the leadership of Mr. Miler, rendered the anthem "0 how manifold are Thy gifts 0 Lord' with taste and precision. They also led the service in a most reverent manner. The decorators were :—Chancel, Mrs. T. H. Corbert ; Mrs. Winter, Miss Davies; chancel screen, Mrs. Savage, Mrs. Harrison, Mrs. Jolley, Miss L. Evans ; pulpit, Miss M. Rigbey ; lectern, Mrs. Lline (Columba, Ceylon) and Mrs. Thomas; font, Mrs. Gorton and Miss Glover ; rest of the Church, Mrs. Daniels, Mrs. Gee and Misses Green, Catterall, Welch, Speakman,. Harrison, Cooper, and others. The ladies were ably assisted by the follow- ing gentlemen :—Messrs. Thomas, Daniels, Bradshaw, and Milner. The collections were in aid of the Curate and Lay Readers 'Fund. After the completion of the services, the truit will be distributed to the sick and poor.

Mr. James Dodds presided

The harvest festival services at the Goltorne-street I Baptist Church were continued on Sunday, the Rev. H. Dannico being the preacher both moraing and evening. ' Mr. Dunnico in the morning pri ached upon the parable of the talents. During the service the choir rendered the anthem "The eyes of all wait upon Thee," Mr. A. Mansell and Mr. C. Nurse taking the principal parts. In the aLernoon there was a musical service by the Excelsior Glee Party. Mr. Ben Few presided and Mr. John Upson gave an address on " Responsibilits," and esid that there was a tendency now adsys to shirk responsibility for easy living. _Ea the evening the Rev. H. Manic° took his text from Judges xvi. 20. He said that the story of Samson was a legend with half a truth in it. He believed that Samson lived and was a very strong man but he did not believe that his strength was through his hair 1 eing unshorn. Samson was a Nazarite and a Nazarite was one who bad taken es solemn vow that he would hold from fleshly lusts'. He had also vowed that he would separate himself from strong drink. A Nazarite was oee,thtn, who refused to corrupt the springs of his man- hood. lie was a man who woeld never allow luxury or indulgence to poison or weaken himself. He was a man who lived a clean and wholesome open life; a man who tried to live the life tht God intended he should live. Were they surprised that be had immense bodily forces? All the same Samson was not an ideal man in his character. He eas simply a msn who had developed one kind of nature at the expense of another. And yet men worshipped men who were nothing hut muscle. He was sick to death of that hero worship of the foot- bal„1 and other professional classes. Si e were woishippineb animal strength and bru:e force, but he thought that we were improving in those things and to-day it was not the wrong in body that impressed himself EO much upon the nat'on, but the man of brain, mind and character. How far were we to-day guilty of Samson's conduct and other kindred things that lei to such terrible havoc in the man's life? The cause of Samson's downfall was bccause he began to live a sensual life which sapped his manhood, and a life which n.ight have ended in glory ended in comparative shame. We were following exactly into that sin—extravagances indulgence, luxury and a reckless control of the appe- tites. Was there not a tendency to waste money and o her things upon worthless things and neglecting the higher and the holier things upon which alone manhooi could ee built ? The reason Ceristians did not impress the world as they ought was because they bad stopped short of sacrifice, and they had ne, er given the sheddirg of blood. Continuing he said he did not know what their opinion might be about theatre going. Whether they believed it in or not,the narrowest minded Puritan was bound to admit that there was all the differencein the •sorld between a good theatre—where the dramas of the greatest minds were presented and their greatest thoughts set before the people—and a cheap shoddy music hall. And yet to-day the music hall was gradually elimin ding the theatre. Some of the men who per- formed at the halls commanded £2O, £3O, £5O, £lOO and even .£2OO a night and they had not got as many brains as we could put into a teaspoon. They were just as far removed from artistes like Forbes Robertson, Martin Harvey and Gertrude Elliott as a man scribbling upon the pavement for coppers and a great artist. Referring to the temperance cause he said that he thought the day had gone by when only the teetotaler could take part it' temperance work. No man would assert that if we abolished drunkenness we would remove poverty from purely an economic point of view, but if we had a sober democracy, in five years we would have England more like the Kingdom of God. Mr. John Burns had said "that we could not expect him to bring in a new heaven and a new earth with'. .E 200,000 a year. What about the .£3,f30,( 00 a week we were spending on drink and gambling and other things?" Those things were just akin to Samson's case. He thought that the great peril of this nation and of other great commercial nations was greed. A hundred years ago the income of this country was £10,000,000 per etnnum ; to-day it was .£1,800,000,000. The population during that period had only increased holt as fast. If the men of a hundred years ago had foreseen that mar- vellous prosperity they would have predicted a paradise where no-one would have perished with hunger. And yet it was not so. Notwithstanding our enormous gain ire. wealth our life was hardened; competition was keener; the burden of life was greater; the rich were becoming richer; the poor becoming poorer, and poverty was taking a sordid form which it never bad in those days. The cause of ril that was greed. What did men care so long as they could corner cotton and make a combine of wheat and hoard up their thousands ? We found peers of the realm—men of genius, although their genius was somewhat erratic—denouncing measures which were trying to elevate, and threatening to crush and kill those measures. And yet those men would spend £2,000 a year on their wine bills. When he thought of those men dictating to the country and to other people as to what should be done and what was not to be done, he found it difficult to flni words in keeping with the dignity of a Christian pulpit to express what he thought. Later he referred to the recent "war scare." He said that some people could not sleep very comfortably in the night for fear that the enemy would come. Nine out of ten of the people who engineered that scare did not believe it. He believed that the engineers of the late war scare were exactly the people who engineered the late Boer war. We must remember that we and the Germanoilrere friends. We had various connections with them—family and commercially—and there was a great bond of sympathy between those who called themselves by the name of Jesus Christ. We had had international visits and only a few weeks ago Baptist ministers went over to Germany and received a hearty welcome. The only thing we had got from the scare was that we had been humiliated in the sight of. the entire civilised world. The very fact that we could be worked up into a war scare showed that we were losing that spirit of strength which was a characteristic of this country. Dread- noughts would never save a nation. Could they point to one single nation in history that had fallen because it lacked the military spirit? Nearly every nation had fallen because it bad over developed and magnified the importance of the military spirit. To hear some people talk, we could only learn to be a patrsot by learning to shoot. The best defence of an Englishman's home was not to teach men to shoot, but to put the fear of God in their hearts. A lot of that talk was bastard patriotism A man could be a good soldier and a bad patriot. A man in whom the animal nature was developed made a good soldier, and yet he might not have an ounoe of patriotism. Patriotism was not to destroy men's lives, hut to save them. He hated the cant and humbue that to be a patriot we must become professional murderers. He wanted them as Christ;an men and women to stand out against the spirit of militarism, which was threaten- ing to crush our country. Lord Rosebery was not always wise, but he was some time ago when he said that " if this piling up of armaments went on we shoald revert hack—speaking of the whole of Europe-'-into a state of semi-barbarism or a terrible catastrophe." All that crying for Dreadnoughts and for National Service was all wrong. We had lost our faith in God. The invasion we needed to fear was not the invasion of the Germans but of gambling and drunkenness, a corrupt Press and a faithless Cnurch. Against those invasiors Dreadnoughts were helpless and Territorials useless.. If we wanted to save England we must bring her back to God sad no nation that had trusted '1 God had ever perished. Peace must ever be the symbol of the Christian. Jesus Christ knew the human heart and when He said "1 hey that take the sword shall perish with the sword" He spoke words of profound truth To parents he said that if they wanted their sore' lives to be honest they should keep their sons out of the Army. The spirit of militarism was growing in War- rington and elsewhere end the Christian Cnureh must hit it. Concluding he appealed to his hearers to follow Christ.

ARREARS OF MAINTENANCE.—John Longshaw Whittaker, Orford-lane, a greengrocer, was locked up for arrears of n3aintainanca amounting to .61 14s. 6d. due to his wife. Compla,nant alleged that prisoner was going with another woman.—Prisoner said com- plainant would not let him get on his feet.—The bench informed prisoner that he would have to pay 7s. 6d. weekly until the deficit was wiped off.—Prisoner : That is very near impossible.—lt was decided to adjourn the case for four weeks to see if prisoner would pay the money.

EGLECT or PAM fix.—Frank Moreland, Foster- s'reet, was charged with neglecting to provide for his family whereby they became chargeable to the Warrington Union.—Mr. Shearer said prisoner's wife had beea chargeable to the Union for some months. Pr isoner was a man about 23 years of age_find was not working but he helped his mother who had two shops. He had not spent a penny on his wife and he had been warned by the Board. There was now only one course opened that was to press the case. He (Mr. Shearer) and the clerk to the Guardians had seen prisoner but he h3d taken no notice and would not attempt to get work at dl.—Prisoner was sentenced to a month's imprison- ment.

GOLBORNE-STREET BAPTIST CHURCH.

DEPARTED MEMBERS.

The PREATER n ade feeling allusion to the heavy loss sustained by the Society during the recess by the death of thrte of its most prominent members—the Lord President of the Council (Councillor J. J. Neild), the Secretary for War (Councillor W. B.Roberts), and Mr. C. Roddy (Nationalist Member for Est Mayo). The Premier concluded by moving that the House record on the minutes their deep sense of the loss sustained. and that an expression of deep sympathy be forwarded to the widows and families of the respected late memberp.

THE

PICTURES OF THE WEEK.

" NIMROD "

AT THE TEMPLE PIER.

The " Nimrod," the little craft in which Lieutenant Shacklcton and his mcn sailed far the South Pole, is lying at Temple Pier on the Thames Embankment. She was stripped of her masts and rigging to enable her to pass under the bridges, and once s).e has been refitted she is to be thrown open for public inspection at a charge of oae shilling per hcad, the proceeds to be handed over to charity. This is the first ozcasion on which Londoners have had an opportunity of inspecting a famous exploring ship at their very cors, and naturally the liveliest interest is being taken in the now historic vessel.

- KING MANOEL.

Gossip is busy just now with regard to the matrimonial intentions of King Manoel of Portugal, the most eligible bachelor in turope. The name of His Majesty, who is to pay a visit to this country in November, is being assiduously associated with that of Princess Alexandra.

A PRIME MINISTER'S SALARY.

HOTEL MANAGER WITH

Mr. Henri Pruger, the well-known manager of the Savoy Hotel, has just resigned his position and sails for New York to take up his duties as Manager of a new restaurant there. He will receive the salary of a Cabinet Minister, and his commission will bring his income up to a sum approaching 10,01:10 a year.

LORD DENBIGH'S SEVEN DAUGHTERS.

Lord and Lady Denbigh-have received many con—gratulations on their silver wedding, an event which has been suitably commemorated at Newnham Paddox, the ances:ral seat of the Fie'dings, in the parish of Monk's Kirby, near Rugby. Our photograph of the silver wedding-day group shows Lord and Lady Denbigh and their seven daughters. The coming of age of Lady Mary Fielding, who is standing to the left of Lord Denbigh, was celebrated at the same time.

HEAD COVERED WITH SORES.

NATIONAL BAND FESTIVAL. Crosfield's Win Third Prize.

Warrington was greatly interested in the national hand festival at the Crystal Palace on Saturday, for high hopes were entertained that Crosfield's band, who came in second last year, would gain till m ich-coveted championship. As events proved, the It cal band had to be content with third place, but this.w as.a creditable achievement, for the contest was keener than probably any that hive preceded it. Fifty thousind . musical enthusiasts were present .at the Cr 3 Pdlace, sitting hour after hour and hear.ng the same pit.ce performed over and over again, while the massed band concert in the evening was kesieged with eagtr listeners. One hundred and fifty bands, with nearly 5,000. per- formers, competed in one or oiler of the nine sections into which the festival was divided. Taken as a whole, the number of Northern bands emu- peting was smaller than usual this year, owing, it was stated, to the depressed condition of the cotton trade, the result of which was the Lancashire lands entering were reduced to ono-half, only thirtern being present. These included, however, the best ban& in the district —Shaw, Goodshaw, I rwell Springs, Win a tes, Perfection Soap Works, and Foden's being entered into the championship section. Two of the remainder were in the concert, and there were five others. The previous winners of the championship, which carries with it, the possession of the thousamil guineas trophy and a cash prize, Iv. re :-1900, Denton Original; 1901, Lee Mount; 1902, Black Dike ; 1903, Besses o'-th'-Barn ; 1901, Hebburn Colliery; 1905 and 1903, Irwell Springs; 1906-7, Wingates, the two last-named being the only bands that have won the trophy twice. The band which wins the trophy the first time secures a bronze medal for each of its memliers, the one which wins it twice a silver medal, and the one which win 3 it thrice a guild medal, but the trophy can never be won outright. Tile test piece was a selectiost.from Wagner's "Flying Dutchman. There appeared to be a consensus of opinion among the experts that Shaw and Perfection performers were at the top of the list,and the iritics awaited the choice of the adjudicators —Lieutentant Charles Godfrey, Mr. James Ord Hume, end Mr. James Brier—with no small interest.

The other contests were, of course, of minor importance, but it was a treat to hear the massed bands' performance, at the close of which the prize- winners were announced. The b i ands taking part n this concert were :—Batley Old, Black Dyke, Clydebank Burgh, Foden's Motor Waggon Works, Goodsbaw, Irwell Springs, King Cross Subscription, London Prize, Perfection Soap ' vrorks and Wga intPs Temperance, the conductors being Mr. J. Henry Iles, Mr. S. Cope, aud Mr. W. Nutt all. _

has lasted quite long enough. An old world lawn and garden lie on the south side of the hall, and enough remains to show us how they looked in distant Stuart days. Here are vases for flowers, shaded walks and terraces, moss grown walls, strange .carved animals and a fine old sundial, and here rests the old font that once adorned the Chapel. The Civil War made sad havoc of Utkyn- ton Hall, and little effort has been made to restore it. Its treasure, by the way, is said to have been hidden in the forest and never recovered. The stained glass adorns Vale Royal, and only the shell that held the departed glory remains. Yet it is abundantly worth a visit.

SUMMER PILGRIMAGES.

UTKYNTON HALL AND BEESTON CASTLE.

With the shortening days we shall perforce

map out some shortened pilgrimages and find our interest nearer home, but before the summer's wanderings are quite over and to show the timid pilgrim how much can be crowded into a generous afternoon we pro- pose to take him upon a long spin through some ,of the most richly interesting of Cheshire's fine highways. We enter our happy country by the Tarporley-road and our first objective is to be the High Billinge and Utkynton Hall.

===== 0002601_19091002_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
STRONGHOLD

A PRE-HISTORIC

The Billinge is a familiar landmark to all who have passed through mid-Cheshire. The highest, or next to the highest point in the Delamere hills, it lifts its tree-crowned summit high and alluring above the plains. Strangely enough this high point is not even marked upon the ordinance Survey map; but it is a spot of peculiar interest. The circular mound on the top is certainly a tumulus for so carefully rounded a hillock can hardly be the work of nature, and the numerous squared stones now built into an encircling wall may easily have formed part of a fortress. These traces of our early progenitors are very interesting and we would give much to be able to lift the veil and see them as they swarmed over the great ant-hills in which they found their protection and their home. We add our

===== 0002601_19091002_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
BEESTON CASTLE.

We do • not stop at Tarporley for we are speeding towards an object which crowns and dominates our landscape—the old castle of Beeston. The fortress is the most promi- nent object in the landscape for many miles around. The crag on which it stands rises more than 500 feet above sea level, on three sides entirely precipitous, and on the fourth approached by a sloping grassy hill-side. We ride round it on the road that skirts the foot and then begin the ascent that leads us up the grossy slope past the protecting walls

===== 0002601_19091002_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
BEESTON TOWERS. THE HOME OF MR. JOHN NAYLOR.

speculations to the rest; and after a quiet enjoyment of the fine scenery descend\ to trace our way to Utkynton Hall. The village of Utkynton is perched high on the hill and does not detain us, but below in the valley is an old building which it is worth a fairly long pilgrimage to see, the ancient hall of Utkynton. At present it is used as a dairy farm and many of its former glories have faded, but there is enough to attract the most careless traveller and no caller will go disappointed away. The occupier receives us most kindly and my fellow-pilgrim and I revel in the enjoyment of the gardens, the lawn and the big old building. _ .

But before mentioning points of interest we must refresh with a few points of information.

THE FOREST MOS.

Nearly all the history of the romantic region of Delamere centres round this old house, for it was the home of the Dones the keepers of the forest. The" Forestership " was a big thing in early days. Originally vested in four families it passed in the 12th century to Ralph de Kingsley who after the manner of the times held it by the tenure of a horn.

This horn the grand forester wore at his side Wheneier his liege lord chose a hunting to ride ; " By Sir Ralph and his heirs for a century blown, It passed from their lips to the mouth of a Done. We are transported at a bound to the bad old feudal days as we read the list of the privileges of the forest, keeper. Some day we may recall them just to show how far we have travelled in social usage and reform. For the present we only mention one or two to enable us to conjure up some of the grim scenes the old walls of Utkyntoil may have witnessed. The Forest kings rulei supreme in their own sphere and had power of life and death within the area of the forest. And they exercised their right. They hanged men first and then argued to justify the deed after- wards. They had a retinue of under-foresters whom they could billet upon the farms of their- tenants. They had the right to all fallen timber, birds, swarms of bees and the right shoulder of every deer hunted or caught in the forest. All smaller animals were theirs and every waif and stray found in the forest became one of their vassals.

and towers to the summit. These towers will serve to start us in the brief historical quest without which it is quite impossible to enjoy an ancient building.

===== 0002601_19091002_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DONES OF UTKYNTON.

The family of the Dones appears as Forest rulers in the 12th century and they figure in the pages of history for fully six hundred years. 'Vigorous fighting men were these Dones of Utkynton and stbod in the forefront of many a battle. And a rough breed they were. Bloodshed, whether on battlefield or in the dark glades of the forest,was the spice of their blustering lives and they ruled the rude countryside with a sword seldom free from bloodstain.

Their crest which may still be traced in the stonework was a shield and arrows. And to complete it the motto " Omnia mei Dona Dei."—All that is mine is the gift of God.

This motto is just superb. The Dones, plunderers of the peasantry, forest tyrants, after building a mansion by wholesale robbery carve upon it a motto to proclaim to the world that it is the gift of God. • The biggest rogues seem to have always taken the most religious crests, as witness that of the next neighbour of the Dones, Sir Piers Dutton. This potentate after robbing the religious houses and charities in shame- less fashion built Dutton Hall out of the pro- ceeds and carved over its portals. " P.D. who thanketh God for all." What things our rich ancestors did! And what things our poor ancestors endured

===== 0002601_19091002_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
UTKYNTON HALL.

But it is time to describe the ancient hall. It is a big rambling building of irregular appearance, the outside being mostly of 17th century work. Much of it plundered by the Royalists in the Civil War,it was not fully rebuilt. Internally we find many things to linger over. In the centre of the great hall rises a huge trunk of oak on which the floors and roof timbers rest. Tradition has it that the original house was built round the growing tree, and there is every probability that this was so. A huge fire place with ingle nooks help us to picture the ball in its distant early days when the fierce owners held court here and gave sentence on the poor shivering

===== 0002601_19091002_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
A REMINISCENCE OF THE CRUSADERS.

These walls were built on the lines of Saracen strongholds, and may have been modelled upon those of Constantinople. Thereby hangs a tale. An earl of Chester who went out on Crusade to the Holy Land spent considerable time in fighting the Saracens and knew by painful experience the value of the double walls joining round towers from which arrows or molten lead could be freely poured. And about the year 1220 he put up these rings of masonry. Yes, these old walls have stood for nearly seven centuries. They are forlorn enough at present and so quiet as to give the idea that they were built for ornament. Perhaps it may invest the place with a little life if we recall a few of the men who have lived and struggled here in a stormy past.

===== 0002601_19091002_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME ANCIENT HISTORY.

The first owner of Beeston Castle, of whom we know very much, was Randle Blundville, who succeeded to it in 1187, and who was one of the Barons who stood over King John at Runnymede to enforce the sealing of the Magna Charta. After his death Henry 111. seized it, and a few years later it was snatched by Simon de Montfort. It was this Simon who called the assembly in which we first see our modern Parliament and who made England

A land of settled government, A land of just old and renown. But the castle came back into royal hands and was the scene of hustle and confusion on, many a memorable day during the Border Wars, for Llewellyn, the Welsh prince, had arisen and kept the Border Castles on the stretch of anxious watching for many A long year. But Llewellyn fell, and Edward, to appease the conquered people, hit upon the expedient of making them a promise. They were to have a prince who should be a native of Wales, who never spoke a word of English, and whose life and character none could quarrel with. He fulfilled the promise when he held up in his arms a tiny baby, his infant son born at Carnarvon Castle, and the first Prince of Wales.

With that happy event Beeston's occupa- tion, like Othello's, was gone, and for a century we hear nothing of it. It next figures as the last and temporary stronghold of Richard the Second. Poor weak Richard supplanted by Bolingbroke came back from Ireland to find his kingdom taken from him, but some loyal Cheshire men became his body guard and kept watch over him by day and night. Because of the loyalty of the county and the impregnable nature of the fortress the kings treasure and jewels were carried here for safety. But the garrison surrendered and Richard passed beneath the high walls on that sad journey to his death which Shakespeare has so pathetically described.,

===== 0002601_19091002_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
A WARRIOR OF NINETY.

In Queen Elizabeth's time the castle had lost much of its glory, but was still an inhabited stronghold. Of its owner in those spacious days we must not fail to say a wordi Sir George Beeston had done brave service at the siege of Boulogne and had seen enough of fighting to satisfy an ordinary man. But 'when at the age of ninety he heard of the coming of the Armada the fight- ing spirit 'stirred within him and he took command of the Dreadnough (famous name!) one of the foufships that in the fore- front of the battle broke the Spanish line. For his valour he was knighted by Queen Elizabeth, and lived to enjoy the honour a dozen years. His altar tomb at Bunbury tells all these things in elegant Latin and says that he was aged 102 years when he ceased to serve his country. There is anther century of history to glance over; but my fellew pilgrim is hungry and the kettle is boiling at the gate house below. We are to have tea' on the greensward and the reader must wait if he would know the rest. A.M.

===== 0002601_19091002_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, M P.

ABOUT WHISKEY AND TOBACCO.

So much attention has been given to the Land Clauses of the But (get that its other features have beQl almost forgotten. The working people appear to be paying their extra taxation quite joyfully, indeed with a grim satisfaction, under the impression that the dukes and other landowners are being severely hit. With the latest concession which the Chancellor has given to the land- owners, this class is not only not going to have to pay more in the way of land taxes, but as Mr. Asquith said at Birmingham "I am quite prepared to maintain and to defend the proposition that the owner of agricultural land, in so far as it is naturally and economi- cally applied to agricultural purposes, will be as well off or,even better off in the matter of taxation after this Budget is passed than he is at the present moment."

===== 0002601_19091002_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
IHE BEST PARTS.

If the big landowners are going to be better off under this Budget, then somebody else is going to be taxed to raise the ,E 13,000,000 the Chancellor requires. In the matter of increase in the Income Tax, the Super tax, and the Death Duties, the rich are going to contribute about one-half of the extra taxa- tion. Although the popularity of the Budget is due to the land clauses, which are not worth a penny to the revenue this year, I have, ever since the Budget was introduced, looked upon the Income Tax and Death Duties proposals as the best part of the Budget from the point of view of producing revenue. These proposals entitle the Budget to receive the support of every person who wants to tap seams of wealth for social reforms.

THE WORKMAN AND THE " MISSIS."

The working men are paying the spirit duty and the tobacco duty without complaint although it is evident that some of them have had to reduce their oon sump tion . An increase of prices was bound to have that effect. A working man's means are so limited that he cannot afford to spend more than a certain slim weekly upon his personal luxuries. If the price of his tobacco and whiskey is advanced by sixpence a week he cannot get that extra pocket money from the " tnissis," but must reduce the volume of his consump- tion to within his old expenditure. EXTRAORDINARY SOBRIETY.

The decline in the consumption of spirits has been extraordinary. The reduction in the revenue from spirits in the four months, May to August, 1909, as compared with May to August, 1908, was £2,489,000. The revenue from spirits for the four months, May to August. 1908, was £6,131,000, so that a reduc- tion of £2,489,000iis equal to 40 per cent. of revenue, and over 55 per cent, in the quantity of spirits consumed. If there were not factors in the taxation of spirits, other than the yield of revenue, such a result as this would be the most striking condemnation of bad statesmanship ever brought home to a Chancellor of the Exchequer. There will have to be an enormous increase in spirit drinking during the next six months if the Chancellor does not find himself at the end of the year with as large a deficit on this item as he expected a surplus. But if we look at this question from other points of view than merely revenue raising we may find some satisfaction in the great decline of spirit drinking. If reduction is going to be permanent, then the Chancellor of the Exchequer 'has by accident discovered a most effective way of making the people sober by Act of Parliament. From the point of view of temperance, it is desirable to discourage the use of ardent spirits, oven if the consumer resorts to lighter alcoholic beverages. I don't expect to carry Scotch- men with me in -that opinion. THE CASE OF TOBACCO.

Something of the same result has happened in the case of tobacco, although the decline in consumption is not so marked. In the four months, May to August of this -year, there has been a decrease in the amount of tobacco taken out of bond of 3,680,190 lbs. This means that during the period named 58,883,040 ounces, or 471,000,000 pipefulls of tobacco less have been smoked than last year. This represents very serious consequences to the manufacturer and the retailer. lam not so much concerned about the manufacturer, who in the tobacco trade is largely a very wealthy monopolist, but such a reduction must have hit the retailer very hard, and he, in most cases, is a man who has a very hard struggle for a living. So far as the experience of these few months goes, it looks as if the profitable taxation of spirits and tobacco had been reached before the increases were imposed. That, at any rate, was the opinion of all previous Chancellors, and both Sir Michael Hicks-Beach and Mr. Austen Chamberlain came to the conclusion, from their experience of tampering with these duties, that any ' further increase of taxes would not add to the revenue.

A FURTHER CONCLUSION. But it seems to me that, this experience carries us to a more general conclusion. The people who have given up drinking whiskey and smoking tobacco have not done so because they have ceased to desire these things. They have done so because they couldn't afford to pay the extra prices for the same quantities. That means, therefore, that the taxable capacity of the working classes has been exhausted, and that any further taxation imposed upon articles they consume will result in diminished consump- tion. I don't think it is unwillingness to pay taxes which is the cause in the great falling-off in the consumption of spirits and tobacco since the increase, but that it is due to sheer want of means to buy the former quantities. THE COMING ISSUE."

At the end of the. present financial year the Chancellor of the Exchequer will find that his best friends have been the Super-tax and the Death Duties, and without these he would find himself without money to pay for Dreadnoughts or pensions. If the experience up to the present of the results of piling up taxes on the poor is confirmed by later results we shall have obtained a very valuable lesson in Tariff Reform. Mr. Balfour has laid down the issue of the next election, whenever it may come, as Tariff Reform versus Socialism. Mr. Balfour's s Tariff Reform is a somewhat nebulous idea, but we are sufficiently aware of his Party's intentions to know that they intend to broaden the basis of taxation by relieving the rich of super-taxes and death duties, and putting the taxes on everything the people use and consume. It doesn't matter much from the econoniic point of view, whether a imor man pays taxes on his tobacco or on his, boots, for in 'either case he is so much the' poorer for the tax, and has so much less to spend. If the people are too poor now to pay

more on tobacco, they are too poor to pay the duties which the Tariff Reformers would impose. So the certain results of Tariff Reform would be, as in the present case of spirits and tobacco, to reduce the volume of trade, and thus inflict widespread ruin on employers and employed. CORRESPONDENCE.

There are only two ways of writing letters just now. The first begins, "Dear Sir Baronet,—l humbly venture to enclose my little account," and ends with a respectful reference to the weather. The second is more important, and must be given in full :

999, Grosvenor Square, W. The Castle, Perthshire. Broaklands, nr Eversbot. Llwyupwllgcb, Dolgelly, Wales. Thomas Towers, Leicester. S.Y. Araminta. (*Strike out five of these.)

You may also miss the beef at Christmas. Yours regretfully, MIDDLESEX. (Including most of the Embankment.) P.S.—I am sending a copy of this letter to the Press—all except the bit about the Lords.

Tariff Reform means Beef at Christmas. (ADvT.)

DUKES. (Hats ',LT please.) I want you to follow me very carefully here, because the position of the Duke is often misunderstood by tactless persons. Suppose that a Duke receives £200,000 a year for his services to the country. Now, at first sight, that seems a lot of money, and hasty people say, "Oh, we must tax this man heavily,he is' so rich." But let us pause and reflect a moment. What does he do with his money? He doesn't bury it or drop it down a well. No, he spends it; and by spending it he gives employment to the poor people of England whom we are all so sofry for. Suppose you take away £lO,OOO from our Duke in taxes. What is the result? Why, he spends £lO,OOO less! That is to say, the poor working people lose £lO,OOO worth of employment. So that in trying to tax a Duke you are really robbing the widow and the orphan, besides hurting th& feelings of a wiseiand kindly legislator! Ilriff Reform means Ilippier

SIR WILLIAM HARTLEY AND CHURCH GIVING. Sir William Hartley presided on Saturday eight over a big meeting of Primitive Methodists at Black- burn in connection with the Primitive Methodist cen- tenary celebrations. Making an appeal for the centen- ary fund which aims at raising £250,000, he said that ha must own to some disappointment with connexional loyalty. All their enterprises had been hold back through lack of money, but all financial difficulties would disappear Hall their people willingly gave a definite share of that incomes. Many Primitive Methodists had told him that they could not bring their minds to support religious and philantropic move- ments with the liberality they luight, simply because tivi had not developed that disposition. What he advocated might take a great deal of doing, but he was convinced it was the only way. It was the greatest joy of his life when he gave everything to God and humanity. (Cheers).

I thought to outwit Heaven, and it has punished me. Like a fool, I thought I could escape • punishment; and Providence has not only punished me, but my child as *ell."

Sho buried her face in her hands, and rocked herself to and fro. The doctor stood for a moment helpless. Then he put his hand on her shoulder.

"Mrs. Dunston," he said, "if all you say is true, Providence has punished you indeed, but He may yet give you a chance to redeem yourself. Your daughter is going now swiftly to be judged. Heaven is merciful, and will see how far she was tempted, how far she was weak. But you—you may yet work out your salvation and the salvation of your daughter." She lifted 'a face that was so wild and white that he fell back from it. " How? How 7 " she cried. "I don't know," he said, "but there is a great deal of work to do in the world. If you could nurse, I could help you. If you are sorry, and wish to work, perhaps I eciald help you.,, She rose unsteadily. "As Heaven is my judge, I will try," she began, and then stopped, arrested by .a movement at the bed. side.

Edward was leaning' over it -speaking to Julia. Her eyes were open, but before the doctor could reach her they had closed again. Edward gave a hoarse cry, and flung him- self face downward upon the bed. The doctor turned from the dead Julia to him, and looked at him with sudden sus- picion. He lay very- still on. his face. He turned him over quickly. " Great heavens!" he cried. A thin stream of blood had oozed out upon his shirt, and then he fell back. sharply. " He has stabbed himself!" he cried. Mrs. Dunston stood rigid for a moment. Then she threw up her arms. " Two lives on my head," she cried, wildly. "Two lives to answer for! Oh! Heaven can never forgive!"

CHAPTER XXV.

It was the end of the summer, and a glori- ous hot morning. The Hall garde-ns were a gorgeous mass of colour—of yellow sunflowers and red hollylicats, of phloxes anti lilies and carnations. Geoffrey stooped over one of the beds, and -plucking one held it out to Olga, who stood by his side. "What is it about a carnation being sweet ? " he asked. "'Carnations .sweet, and so are you. darling." He stood, catching her hand as she held it out for the flower and keeping it between his. "Olga," he added, " when are we going to be married. darlinn 7"

Geoffrey slapped him on the back.. "The sooner the better," he cried. Then, as Dick stared at him, he added: "The so,ner you decide to marry her the sooner Olga will de- cide to marry mo. Hurry her up, old man, and beg for a double wedding. It will wake Kennet up." "Kennet has been woke up enough for one year, I should think," said Dick, "but a wedding—and a double one—will round of things nicely. They want rounding off, Geoffrey. The thing haunts me like a night- mare still, and Julia's death and Edward's suicide—the remembrance still sends me cold."

"But everybody is forgetting . now,,, said Geoffrey. Things are straightening out, -ind even mother is getting quite bright 11,7ain. I was afraid it would make her ill, ;mit she was plucky, wasn't she? She felt she id Olga to look after, and that kept her lig. She had no time to think of herself ust °then, and if it hadil teen for her Olgz. would have been seriously ill, I think. Mother saved her, and herself too. Good old mother She won't approve of Ethel going."

"she won't go far," said Dick, "only next door—over the garden wall, in fact." "The garden wall is a mile off, all the same," replied Geoffrey. "They can't very well pop out and talk over it before break- fast."

Dick laughed a little. They can try," he said.

"I suppose they'll have to," retorted Geoffrey. They had reached the corner of the terrace wall, where the drive was just visible, and suddenly Dick stood still. "Geoffrey," 'he cried, "who is that?"

Geoffrey ' 'looked and raised - -1-11s'. brows sharply. "It's Mrs. Dunston ! What on earth can she want?"

At that moment Mrs. Verriker appeared at the window above them. She opened it and came out slowly..

"Geoffrey," -she said, "I've just had a visit from Mrs. Dunston."

"Well, mother ? "

"She came—to say good-bye, and to ask us to forgive her," she said. "She couldn't see you; the poor thing was too upset—too broken-hearted. She has changed terribly. She doesn't look the same; but, somehow, .I like the change." "She came to say good-bye?" Geoffrey cried. "Where is she going to?" "To London," said Mrs. Verriker. "Her furniture is to be sold and the money given to the vicar; he has, in fact, got charge of the sale. She has given him everything, and is going to London without a penny in her pocket to join some mission in the East End. She says she has many things to wipe out, and perhaps she has." "Perhaps she has," echoed Geoffrey, "but it seems strange to think of her working in a slum. I always feel as if I stood in her shoes —almost as if I had cheated her, poor thing. I always feel that I want to make it up to hes for coming into the estate, and yet I don't know how I can."

"No, you can't," said Mrs. Verriker, "and the work will be the best thing for her. Shit will forget everything in poor people'i troubles. I am glad she is doing that." "Dr. Grey said she would," remarked Ethel. "He told me so."

"Well, now come and have lunch," said Verriker. "I tried to persuade her tc stay, but I coUld not. She could not face ue all again—not yet, anyhow." "Perhaps we may go and see her in her slum," said Ethel, linking her arm in Dick's. "After we are married," replied Dick. "Who says we are going to ho married?" asked Ethel. "I do " said 'Dick.

Ethel 'stood still, looking into his eyes. "When shall it be ? " he asked, gravely.

"Whenever you like," ,Ethel —whierecl back.

And the double' wedding took place before many weeks were' over, while the summer was still in its glory, and before the autumn winds robbed the Hall gardens of their flowers. With the sun pouring full upon her Olga took into her hands, that were clean now, a man's life, and gave her own in ex- change. The two little Cathcarts were, her brides- maids, and the , two boys her pages. Ethel had an equal number, and she knelt - side by side with the girl she had once doubted, and who rose at last, Sir Geoffrey's wife, cleared of all stain and shame. __

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Flcors Castle.—The Prince and Princess of Wales are expected to pay .their long promised visit to the Duke and Duchess of Roxburghe, at Floors Castle, Kelso. The Duke's friendship with the Prince is one of long standing,and he accompanied His Royal Highness on his Colonial tour in the Ophir. The Duchess—formerly Miss May Goelet---is the richest of the three American reigning Duchesses, and is understood to have inherited a fortune of £5,000,C,00 on her father's death in 1901. The Duke first met his wife in London, in 1898, and married her in 1903. The Duke and Duchess have spent a fortune in. restoring their Scottish seat, where Royal guests have frequently been entertained. Queen Victoria visited Floors Castle in 1867, and the King and Queen, as Prince and Princess of Wales, the Duke of Edinburgh, the Duke of Albany, and other Royal personages have also stayed there. The Duke of Connaught. —There is some idea that the Duke of Connaught will succeed Lord Aberdeen as Lord Lieutenant of Ireland. The Duke and Duchess were very popular in Ireland, during the time His Royal Highness was commander of the forces, and in a sense he may be said to belong to Ireland. The Duke takes his title from one of the provinces and one of his Christian Dames is that of the patron saint of Ireland. This be owes to the visit which Queen Victoria and ,Prince Albert paid to Ireland in 1819. The Duke was born on May Ist in the following year. That was the anniversary of the birthday of the Duke of Wellington, from whom the baby prince took his first name, Arthur, and the great soldier Duke was onesof his sponsors. Our. Alleged Summer.—lt seems to be only Ireland, notably the west and south, that has enjoyed a really fine summer, and that at least cannot be counted another injustice to the distressful country. For the rest of the Britisll Islands the 'summer has been. more or less a dismal fraud, with many more wet and unsettled days than are usually our allotted portion. The Mark Tapleys have been saying all along that a glorious time was coming to redress the summer's failure, and, despite the fact that a wet summer usually brings no settled fine autumn, they still promise us a fine October. A second summer they suggest, regardless of the fact that few of us arc aware of having had the first. We must hope it will be so, and if things do not improve next year we can put it down to Halley's comet. Comets invariably get the credit of bringing disasters, and if we have a severe winter, or another bad summer, our celestial visitor will surely be blamed for it. Concerning Superstition.—Although of late years superstition generally has been slowly growing less, there still remains a decided antipathy to the number thfrteen. And this antipathy is almost universal, the Turks especially so disliking the word that it is never used ,by them. With its, the number thirteen is frequently missing from the streets, as it is from rooms in hotels, and cabins on liners, and few hostesses, even in these enlightened days, would venture to arrange their dinner table for thirteen persons. Another super- stition to which many cling is the undesirability of spilling salt, especially when it is being passed from one person to another. The origin of this superstition is veiled in mystery, but this we do know, the Romans certainly believed in it. An Old Country Custom.—ln many parts of the country the custom of "telling the bees " of any ill. fortune, or trouble overtaking the house, is still faith- fully carried out, and the following story bearing on this custom comes from a remote part of Devonshire. A few weeks ago, a farmer there died, and his successor proved to be a person all unvetsed in the habits and customs of bees. Again and again he was warned that unless the bees were told of the farmers' death, they would resent the omission,. but the sceptical one only laughed and said he was willing to run all siske in this direstion. In due course the estate was sold, and the bees came" under the hammer." When however the purchaser made his way, a little later, to the hives, it was found that not one bee had remained to be sold or "taken."

Al-dOint of Fashion.—Judge Emden recently remarked that there ought to be a Court of Fashion to settle dress disputes. "In view of the many eccentr'cities of fashion" he said, "there ought to be a court of arbitra- tion where all fashion eccentricities would be under- Stood." The particular eccentricit3 which Judge Emden could not understand on this occasion, was the difference between an Empire and a Princess gown, the customer saying she had ordered the one and been supplied with the ether. This idea of a Court of Fashion is not new. as it has been tried in Fiance, and its being adopted here would be generally welcomed by the dressmaking firms in London. They say It is easy for dissatisfied customers to exaggerate any slight defects in a new garment and by making these a conspicuous exhibition in Court, they can generally persuade the judge to decide in their favour irrespective of the general merits of the case.

Sleeves —So far there is little new to chronicle hi the way of sleevss. AU are tig'itly fitting from shoulder to wrist, th 3 only difference lying in the arrangements of the fabr'c. The woman possessing thin arms clings to the pleated or racked varieties, while the " chubby " arm is clothed in plainer an't more simple fashion. One point-, however, calculatea to interest and delight the impecunious, is the Per" mission granted them by leading modistes, to have sleeves of different fabric to the remainder of the gown They must match precisely in colour, but the material may be exactly what the renovators choose. How to Look Taller thin we are.—A generous length of limb as we well know, is invaluable in the world of modes, but a point so many less-favourei girls andr women apparently fail to grasp, is the possibility o making themselves appear more lengthy than theY really are. For instance, much may be gained by having dresses and costumes of one material onlY; preferably in light tones, with all lines running up ana down ; by avoiding the square yoke, also belts of con- trasting hue; by selecting tall-crowned and somewhat narrow hats, sharply upturning at one side; by never wearing ruffles or tall flutings • and by having sit indoor gowns built upon Empire lines. Thq hair shonlo be dressed high and only slightly puffed out at the sides, and as far as possible "tall colour schemes " such' as pink, white, cream, ohampigne, and light green, should considered. Brown shortens, so also doei black. Further, shiny fabrics are better than dull ones. the former reflecting the light making the wearers appear taller. The " Cap " Coiffure.—To turn, almost in a single day, from the be bouffant coiffure, with its curls and waves, to a perfectly sleek head, the hair being simPII drawn to the nape of the neck, and brought loosely. forward " cap ' fashioti, is a serious business. 49/ course when abundant" natural material " falls to our lot, or abundant pin money with which to purchase " acquired " tresses, one's path is eased considerably, but for the less endowed the present sharp veer roold is indeed an anxious affair. The necessary Hal straight fringe is invariably "acquired," few girls an' women posseising courage to cut hair they have se carefully cultivated and tended during the past two or three sears. Mrs. Langtry's Novel.—Lady de Bathes promised novel "All at Sea" has now been published by Messrs. Hutchinson. In this, the author, formerly Mrs. LangtrY describes the adventures of a pretty woman who gee° on a voyage with her husband. The chief feature of the story is, that by mutual arrangement they keep apart on the boat, and pretend not to know one another, she Posing as a widow, and he as a bachelor. The complies" tions that arise, as may be imagined, are very enter' taming. • MADGE'

Stockton Heath.

FUNERAL OF MRS. CARTWRIGHT.—The funeral took place in the Hill Cliffe graveyard on Monday afternoon of Mrs. Cartwright, wife of Mr. James Cartwright, licensee of the Birch and Bottle, Whitley. The chief mourners were Mr. Jams Cartwright (husband), Messrs. H. and S. Cross (brothers). Messrs. Jennings and Turner (brothers-in-law), Mr. john Girt wright (uncle). Mr. James Cartwright (cousin), Mr. W. Cross (nephew), and Messrs. T. Boscow and J. Waldron representing the Warrington and District Licensed Victuallers' Associa- tion. The 1-earers were Messrs. James Barker, L. Mather, T. H. Howard and William Sparks. Mr. James Whitley, of Stockton Heath, conducted the last rites.

FATAL FALL DOWXSTAIRS.-Mr. T. Ridgway held an inquest at Stockton Heath, on Thursday en the body of Margaret M`Leish, wife of -Robert M`Leish, ironmoulder, Leonard-street, who died on Wednesday as a result of falling down- stairs. The husband stated that he went to bed early on Tues:--ay night, leaving his wife in the kitchen. At different times she drank a good deal. Early on Wednesday morning he heard a fall, and on proceid;ng downstairs found his ,wife lying at the bottom bleeding from the mouth. He went for a doctor, but death took place before his arrival. There was no stair-rail. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," and recommended that houses of this description should have a stair-rail put in.

HARVEST FESTIVALS.--The harmst thanksgiving services were held at the Wesleyan Chapel on Sunday. Corn, fruit, flowers, vegetables had been sent by friends in abundance and the decoraticns were skilfully carried out by the willing tend of ladies of the congregation. The preacher in the morning was the Rev. T. Gunnell. The special music vs as a very attractive feature. In addition to anthems by the choir special hymns were also sung. At the evening service the Rev. Thomas Gladding preached and spoke upon the lessons that. could be derived from the harvest. Both morning and evening discourses were listened to with the closest attention to large congregations. Mr. George Weaver presided at the organ at both services. _ _ _ _

The harvest fesaval service was held at the Church Mission on Wednesdly evening. The nnetuary was tastefully decorated with corn, fruit, flowers and vegetables, Sze. by the Misses Tinsleys, Miss Gilbert, Mesdames Gilbert, DucLill and Pennington, and great credit is due to them. SLAUGHTER OF DISEASED ANIMALS.

The St. Alban's Rural District Council forwarded this resolution for adoption :—" That in the opinion of is Council the payment of compensation for the slaughter of diseased animals should be made out of the Imperial funds." It was added that "if compensa- tion is to he paid out of local rates' districts of an aviculture' character, and therefore the least able to Lear the expence may be heavily burdened for the benefit end advantage of large urban areas. As ,the question is of great import.nce to large towns the expense should be spread over the whole country." Mr. Antwis proposed that the Council should support the resolution. FoR ECONOMY.

Mr. Clement W. Swetenham, provisionr 1 secretary of the proposed Ratepayers' Defence Association, wrote asking the help and sympathy of Guardians and Coun- cillors towards the movement. The programme of the association was stated to include opposition to the return of all candidates not in favour of economy and the flndlng and supporting of candidates willing to accept office on the County Council, and who would pledge themselves to reasonable economy irrespective of party politics. Mr. Linaker said be fully agreed with the proposition as there had been many complaTnts. of the expensive way in which the County Council carried out their work and he heartily supported the idea of en association for the defence of ratepayers. ' Mr. Hallows said many people had gone up to him and asked him about the high rates and be had told them to go to the County Council. They were the sinners. (Laughter). Mr. Norman said he did not think it was a proper thing for that Board to support such a resolution. Mr. Piling moved an amendment that the letter lie on the table. *to Mr. Norman seconded.

The Board then voted on the matter. Seven voted for - the amenernent and eleven for the proposal to welcome the suggested form of ratepayers defence in the county of Cheshire.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON,

SHOP WORKERS. MR. J. A SEDDON, M.P., AND GOVERNMENT PROPOSALS.

A meeting of shop assistants and clerks was held on Sunday night at the Grand Theatre, Manchester, in connection with the National Federation of Shop Workers and Clerks and the Council of the Shop Assistants' Union. Mr. R. J. Davies, of the Co-opera- tive Emplwes' Union, presided. Mr. Francis moved a resolution which- stated that the Shops Bill drafted by the present Government would not satisfy the orgsnised employees' until amended to reduce the hours shop and office workers to £0 per week, inclusive of meal hours. Mr. Seddon, M.P., said he welcomed the Shops' Bill because it proposed for the first time legislation dealing with the hours of shop workers. The provision that an assistant should not work for more than 60 hours in a week exclusive of meal times was an advance on the proposals made in Sir John Lubbock's Shop Hours' Bill. Such an enactment was necessary. In Glasgow they had an early closing order, but while the shcps were closed the assistants were kept working, some of them SO and 100 hours a week. While they criticised the Government's bill they recognized its fundamental advantage, and expressed their appreciation of it. The sanitary regulations included in the bill were also valu- able, and they would gladly accept the bill subject to modiccation suggested in the resolution. The resolution was carried.

" Consistent Birmingham."

Sir,—Some three or four weeks ago I heard Mr. lifosman (I think that was the gentleman's name) make a "Tariff Reform(?)" speech at Bridge Foot, and at question time asked him how Imports were paid for. He admitted the economic truth that they were paid for by Exports. Last night I asked Mr. Paton, who is with the Conservative van, who was speaking at Latchford, the same question, hut he said it was a fallacy to suppose that Imports paid for Exports. Now the telegraphic address of the " Tariff Reform" headquarters, at Birmingham, is "Consistent," but apparently the missionaries from this ass,.ciation do :Jot understand the meaning of the word. Mr. Paton promised us another speech at Bridge Foot, to-night, Saturday. Is it too much to ask him to give us his exposition of how Imports are paid for ?—Yours truly,

"The Mount," Grappenhall October Ist, 1909.

A. H. RATHBONE.
