Warrington and its Advantages The Provision for a Dock.

Sir,---Warrington has advantages as an industrial centre in excess of most towns in England. Not only is its geographical position superior owing to its close proximity to tho chief port of England, viz., Liver- pool, but it has the great advantages of water and ettil and nearness to coal fields.

A fact also which lie a most important one and should not be lost sight of is that on the Ship Canal goods can be earned, owing to the arrangements which have been effected and entered into by the Cor- poration of Warrington, at a third less lost. than they can be carried to Manchester.

One point, however, which I desire to raise is, do we as a town possessing these considerable advantages which be at our doors make the best use of them, and also do we grasp the possibilities which lie in front of us? I also desire to point out too, that the industrial and commercial success of a town does not mainly rest upon its geographical position but chiefly upon the foresight and enterprise of its citizens both individually and collectively. Illustrative of this fact I may say that Liverpool and Glasgow do not directly owe their great trade to their geographical position, but mainly to the great foresight, and energy of their citizens. Glasgow particularly is a typical example to Warrington in this respect. Geographically it is not so well situated as Warrington. The Clyde also at Glasgow at one time was not equal to what the Mersey is at Warrington, and to-day is very little wider. But now owing to the great energy and enteeprise of its citizens ocean- going steamers and other large ships are to be daily seen at its doors. My special object in writing is to advocate that Warrington should take the preliminary steps necessary for the provision of- a dock. There are already facilities, exceptional and fortunate ones in this respect, seeing that there is already an available site in what is known as the old river bed, which com- municates with the Ship Canal, has a great quantity of land along its banks and, has railway communication at hand.

It appears to me that the present time is most favourable and opportune for Warrington, to at least take the preliminary steps. Financially the town is in a position to do so. It is n't burdened with debt. Th 3 preliminary steps also should not be expensive. .

may say that I have had several interviews with shippers and manufacturers on this subject and they agree with me that Warrington would' in the long run benefit to a much larger extent 'than most people are aware of.

I may add too that Warrington is certainly pro- gressing. As evidence of this six new works have during the past twd years been commenced in the town.

I could say a great deal more on this subject but I will go so far and say that if the Corporation does not seize the opportune moment and make provision for a dock now it will cost the town considerably more in future than it will at the present time. It is for the Corporation to take the lead.—Wake up War- rington,—Yours truly,

E. ENGLAND

The diversion of wealth out of the channels in which it is spent in luxury into channels in which it is use- fully spent, instead of diminishing the capital of the country will increase it, and the more wealth is kept in useful channels where it is productive the better it will be for the country. An excess of wealth, especi- ally if the possessor does not ray the natural price for it, that is, gives no work or service for it, is likely enough to be sp,ent in luxury, and it requires no encouragement from Mr. Jeans to turn it in that direction. When, therefore, Mr. Jeans commends it as an economic advantage and tries to make the man 'who consumes and wastes the produce which the workers have toiled for into a public benefactor, it is time that a vigorious protest was made against such reasoning and Mr. Jeans called upon to vindicate his strange argument. , CHARLES E. PARKER. Penketh, September 29th, 1979. -- " A Nation in Arms."

Sir,—Will you kindly allow me to criticize some of the opinions, expressed by E.V.W., in your issue of September 25th. In the first place, the writer appeArs to think that the failure of the Navy, in the - play, to prevent a landing by foreign troops is an impossi- bility, and that the episode is a slight to the excel- lence of the Navy: Nothing but 'adniiration of our Bluejackets is expressed in the Play, but the object of the Navy is not to form a cordon round the coast of these islands but rather to defend the interests of the Empire and its trade routes all over the world; and a contingency in which a large portion of the Navy might t e required in distant waters is hr from impossible. Ther- efore the Navy must be free to move, its mobility is its strength, and it can only be free if thi s country is able to defend itself (even if only for a short time). The author of the " Nation in Arms " does not taunt his - countrymen with being an " untrained defenceless mob but is justly critical of the system which prevents them, however Plucky individually, from capably helping their country in case of need. Can E.V.W. deny that 9 tenths of the male popula- tion is a large majority to stand idle while the one, 1. tenth tries to do its duty, by training to defend the homes and families of its neighbours? The efficiency, or inefficiency, of the Territorials iS a bone of contention. All honour to the Territorials!• They do their best. But as long as the system is voluntary it is impossible to ask them to give enough time to make them efficient to meet fully trained troops. The justice of universal training should appeal to every reader of the "Examiner," as opposed to the injustice of asking the patriotic few to do the work of the many shirkers. _

Surely yottr correspondent has not grasped the object of the National Service League when he says that it encourages "not an industrious, inventive, progressive, peace-loving community, but an armed, camp." Is not the main object of the League to insure peace by making an attack on us too risky an experi- ment? And would the fact of giving every young man of 18 years of age, four months in camp and then a fortnight with the Territorials for the -subsequent three years, reduce the country to an "armed camp ? " No one can say that it would. Your correspondent boasts that this country is still inhabited by sane people, but if such statements as that to which I allude, are made, we shall find some difficulty in believing him.—Yours &c., ANTI-WArt.

===== 0002601_19091002_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
An Economic Absurdity.

Sir,—Referring to the letter of Mentor,:or as I must

-now call him Mr—. W. Dampier Jeans, Senior, which appeared in your " neutral " contemporary, this gentleman asks me a number of questions and threatens me with a further batch in the next issue. I will do my best to answer these questions if they are pertinent to the principles of the Budget Land Clauses but if tljey are merely legal questions in regard to the workings of the Act I will refer them back to the legal gentleman who asked them. Meanwhile I desire to refer to the letter over Mr. Jeans own signature which appeared on September 18th. Mr. Jeans finds fault with Mr. The for not explaining to his Warrington audience that the pro- posed Land Taxes would reduce the spending power of the landowners, result in their being unable to maintain their large staff of servants, diminish their 'spending power amongst tradespeople, stop • the flow of money at the fountain head, result in their being less money to go round,less employment and remuner- ation for everybody, and that this reduction would reduce the money spent in travelling, pleasure, and luxury. I may be wrong but to my mind this argument is based on an economic absurdity. Mr. Jeans speaks of stopping the flow of money at the fountain head i.e. the landlords. In like manner the Duke of Devon- shire warned his audience at Haddon not to kill the goose that laid the golden eggs, meaning of course the landlord (that is in this case the man who gets rent from socially created value and not from capital expenditure or effort of his own) is not the fountain head, or the layer of golden eggs at all. The fountain head is the producer, the manufacturer, the worker. He pays rent to the landowner because he has no option since he must have land to live and work upon. If there( was not the convenience of money he would pay his rent in kind, that is hand over part of his labour product, which the, landowner would in his turn hand to his gardeners, gamekeepers, ex., for service rendered. It is clear then that the producer is the fountain head, and not as Mr. Jeans claims the landlord. The landlord is merely a channel into which the labour product is diverted, and the product of labour is consumed by those whose labouf is unproductive, and a depletion of the general stock is the result. Now had this produce which goes in land rent been left in the hand of the producer, the probability is that it would have been spent ia direc- tions where it was productive, and not in luxuries which perish in their use. Do not understand, I would not imply that all pro- duce should be used as capital for further production. It would be a dull world if there were no margin for enjoyment and reasonable luxuries. lam here meet- ing Mr. Jeans on his own ground, wherein he contends that the spendings of the rich are economically desir- able, and that they increase the well-being of the workers. To my mind the error lies iii looking upon work as an end instead- of a means, and hence the careless thinker ignores how labour is applied, and thinks only of its employment. Out ef this error arises false reasoning in other directions. Three examples which run on the same, or on parallel lines may be given. First. That fires are good for trade because they give employment in replacing the burnt struc- ture. Second. That it would be beneficial to the country to shut out by duties foreign manufactured articles even if it cost 10 per cent. more to make them here. Third. That improvement in machinery, is bad because it displaces labour and lessens employment in the manufacture of the article in which the machine is employed.

If is not necessary at the moment- to go into a detailed exposure of these absurdities, and it would be presumptuous on my part to pretend to teach Mr. - Jeans the a. b. c. of economic science, but I am sur- prised that he should be led into such an absurdity as his argument indicates. But inasmuch as the bulk of the titled aristocracy have fallen into the same error it may be necessary to go into further detail when we know exactly where Mr. Jeans stands. Suffice for the

present to affirm that if money or money's value is left in the hands of those who create it through a reduction of land rent (or what comes to the same thing a fairer adjustment of taxation) the retention of this rent charge for land, "naked land," will go to the creation of capital and assist in further production and more productive and increased employment. •

===== 0002601_19091002_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
Captain Crosfield and the invasion " Craze."—A Reply to the Member.

Sir,—Aversion to add to the burdens of our over- worked Parliamentary representative on the one hand constrains me to, pass his letter in your last issue unnoticed; on the other hand, that the " make-ready- after-the-war-has-begun" view should hold the field forces me to reply to the head of our house. Firstly, as to the pacific assurances of our German friends. I agree at once that the average Teuton will ridicule the idea that he or his eountrymen have any desire to shed our blood. I believe, too, that the average German is thoroughly sincere when he states that what he wants is peace so that he may build up his business and .add to his own wealth and that of the Vaterland. But such 'assurances do not in the very slightest shake my firm conviction that if, while Germany becomes as strong on sea as she is on land, we remain only strong at sea and hopelessly weak on land; if, as the play says, we continue to put all our eggs in one basket, a day will come when owing to some conflict- ing interests which may have arisen between the two nations, interests which both consider vit 11, the German Government will feel that -she is sufficiently powerful to force her point and we must either ignominously and-ingloriously surrender or must fight under disadvantages which will prove disastrous. Remember the executive power of the German govepment is far greater thanour own. Thecherished plans and ideals of Germany's rulers are not confided fo the German people, and the carefully thought out schemes do not become evident to the masses till , after they have materialised. Look at the history of modern Germany. In those three wars whereby Germany was welded into a solid nation the great Chancellor and his government did not consult before- hand the wishes or desires of the electorate. Neither in 'GI when we basely deserted Denmark and allowed Schleswig-Holstein to be calmly annexed by Prussia; nor in '66 when the proud house of Hohenzollern overcame the yet prouder house of Hapsburg • nor again in '7O when united Germany poured forth her vast armies with unerring precision and unwavering will into the plains of in belle France—in none of these instances did the average German realise before- hand the policy which the great Bismarck was follow- ing out. Bismarck is dead, buthis traditions live on. And the last great war, the fourth, the most momentous of all, which will enable Germany to step into our shoes is no mere figment of an overwrought brain. If our young men know how to defend their country I have no fear ; as long as they do not we are tempting Providence. Turning to the other point in the letter, my brother seems to think that all this training is going to be wasted because it will not be given to the ones who requi re it most. Now we should all rejoice if it were practicable to give the physically unfit systematic training—let us hope that this may eventually become possible—but to withhold the undoubted benefits of such training from the rest of the community simply because they are not in such urgent need of it as the rest seems neither wise nor kind. According to this argument you have no business paying special atten- tion to the intelligent boys at school and helping them forward as much as possible—you ought to concen- trate your efforts on the dunces and the dullards on the ground that they need educating more than the intelligent ones. As a matter of fact national training would have, as it has in Switzerland, an indirect bearing on the physique of the whole of the yotith of the nation because no lad of any spirit would wish to be rejected as unfit to serve his country. Conse- quently there would be an all-round increased interest taken in manly sport and in gymnastic exercises. Lastly we are charged with endangering the .peace by talk of war. Wild and extravagant talk is to be deprecated on all grounds but the great majority of League speakers cannot be charged with this fault for they are most careful to point out that if disaster comes we can only blame ourselves. " Curse your- selves " says Major Viner " you left your front door open." The people who endanger peace are those who are deaf to the warnings of our greatest soldiers, Lord Roberts, Lord Wolseley, Sir George White, who have fearlessly and emphatically told us again and again that we invite attack through our unpreparedness for war.—l am, yours etc, . GEORGE CROSFIELD.

WARRINGTON ASSESSMENT COMMITTEE.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Assessment Committee was held at the Workhouse yesterday, Mr. J. flillinge presiding. There were also present:— Messrs. A. J. Jolley, J. Evans, W. Gill, W. Furness, R. Bolton, G. Mather. A. Brooks, A. Bottomlev (clerk), R. R. Meacock (valuer), W. H. Woodcock (assistant overseer for Wari ington).

:MESSRS. H.M. 'POCHTS AND CO., LTD.

Mr. Davies, of Messrs.. Humphrey Davies, and Co. London, appottled on behalf of Messrs. FL D. Pochi n and Co., Ltd., against the assessment of their works at Bank Quay at £750 gross, £550 ratable. Mr. Meacock said the increase of the assessment on the old valuation was £l7O, the former ratable value being £3BO. He was not aware that there had been any Increase in the works since the old valuation. On behalf of the firm it was stated that certain branches of the trade had declined considerably, and therefore a large amount Of the plant was not in use. There bad been no extension of the premises and nothing had been done physically to enhance their value. It was contended that there ought to be a reduc- tion instead of an increase of the assessment, and that the ratable value ought not to exceed £250. The matter was referred to the Valuer and Clerk for conferetce with the firm. --:4111 VALUER'S SALARY.

, Committee. The Workingmen's Committee of the Warrington District Nursing Association is doing an excellent

work on behalf -of that worthy institution, and an encouraging statement was submitted at the annual meeting which was held in the Council Chamber at the Toe n Hall on Thursday evening. The total amount raised by the Committee during the past year is £292 Bs. 6d., as agtinst £560 Bs. 3d. in 1909, but the decrease is more than accounted for by the fact that last 30ar a special appeal by the then Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) realized the substantial sum of £356 4s. Gd. The meeting was presided over by the Deputy-Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) and there was a good attendance of delegates from the various worki, including Mr. G. Walker (chairman), Mr. H. Hope (hon. treasurer), and Mr. J. Fargher (hon. secretary). Councillor Roberts (ta easurer of the Nursing Association) was also present. It was announced tlat business in London prevented the attendance of the Mayor. Letters of apology for non-attendance were received from Mr. W. P. Rylands (President of the Committee), and Mr. F. Monks (founder of the Nursing Association). In the course of his letter Mr. Monks said :—lf I cannot attend meetings and help as I used to do, it is not from any waning interest in all the movements that help for the improve- ment of my fellow-townsmen, but because I have to recognize the limitations of my physical strength if I am CO get along with any degree of personal comfort. I have done my full share in the past and must leave it to younger men to carry on the work. lam very glad that you have passed the £250 limit and when the people fully realise what the Nursing Association can do, as an educational influence in the treatment of disease, I feel sure they will not let it want for further financial support. We are only on the threshold of the social amelioration of the people, one of the results of better -education and self-respecting influences at work among them ; progressive, but not revolutionary in the sense of legally robbing any man's "hen roost." MEacitis,in moving the adoption of the accounts, pointed out that the contributions from the ordinary channels showed an advance of £79 on last year. The HON. TREASURER said one or two works had ceased contributing owing to bad trade and others through misunderstanding. He hoped they would soon induce all the works and friendly societies to make collections. Mr. W. P. Rylands was re-elected president and the following were re-elected vice-presidents :—The Mayor (Alderman Forshaw). Alderman Smethurst, Captain Crosfleld, Alderman Burton, Messrs. F. Monks, V. W. Walker, W. Charnock,and Alderman Burton; representatives on the Executive Committee of the Nursing Associatior, Messrs. G. Walker, J. Fargher and H. Hope. The CHAIRMAN said that the committee had now been in existence for four years, with the following financial results :-1906,£159 Os. 9.1.; 1907,t191 195.11 d.; 1908,2204 3s. 9d.; 1909, £282 85.6c1.; total,£B37 125.11 d.; (plus the ex-Mayor's special appeal in 1908 which realized £356 4s. 6d) £1,1a3 17s. sd. In 1905, the year before this committee was formed, £7B 6s. 10d. was raised from the local works, friendly societies, &c. Therefore the committee had justified its existence by a considerable increase in subscriptions. The working- men deserved every praise and credit for their efforts and he trusted that God would long spare them to con- tinue their glorious work. Councillor ROBERTS said the increased contribution from the Workingmen's Committee had gone a long way towards placing the Association not only out of debt but in the position of having a credit balance at the end of the financial year. He paid a warm tribute to the special and generous efforts of Mr. F. J. Lord in the promotion of the operas by Messrs. Crosfield's Amateur Operatic Society, the whole of the proceeds of the performancEs being handed to the committee. He did not know how to express himself adequately as to the character and effect of the kindly consideration which both Mr. and Mrs. Lord had shown to the institution. (Applause). Continuing, Councillor Roberts said he hoped the Association would be so well supported that they would be able to increase the staff when the growth of the town demanded it. He did not think the Association had ever been better staffed than at present. (Applause). Warrington stood exceedingly well among the towns of England in regard to the contributions of workingmen towards institutions of that kind. The other evening he (Mr. Roberts) had a visit from Mr. F. Monks whose interest in the association was as keen as ever and he was cer- tain that as long as he lived Mr. Monks would keep up that interest and do all he could for the success of the institution. (Applause). On the motion of Mr. HOPE, seconded by Mr. WALTERS, a vote of condolence was passed with Mrs. Nield and family on the death of Councillor Nield. • Votes of thanks were passed to Mr. E. Bradshaw (auditor),the Mayor for the use of the Council Chamber, and Alderman Smethurst for presiding. The CHAIRMAN emphasised the value of the work done by the nurses who during the past year had paid 14,499 visits to 631 cases.

BOXING AT THE BARRACKS. Excellent Contests.

With the prospect of a fine entry of fighting men and spirited contests, in which they were not disappointed, an excellent crowd of spectators assembled at the Orford Barracks on Thursthy evening. The occasion was the first meeting of the winter session in connec- tion with the Orford Barracks Boxing Club. The chief event of the programme was the grand ten round contest for club purse and £l.O a side, between Bert Regan, af Warrington, and Jonty Haywood, of Salford. The rounds were of two minutes duration and 6oz. gloves were used. On coming forward for the first round Haywood was seen to be the bigger man. He was also one stone heavier. Nothing of import- ance took place in the first round, both men feeling each others points. The sec:.,nd round saw Haywood having slightly the better of the fight. The third round started the struggle in earnest and Haywood, after making feints at his opponent's body , righted quickly and landed two blows on Regan's face, the effect being visible all throu.eh the fight. It was noticed that Haywood was in by far the better condition and on coming up for each round he was quite fresh whereas Regan seemed somewhat exhaurt,..d. At the sixth round Regan who had been on the defensive practically the whole of the time, assumed the aggresive and by a sadden onslaught got home with some splendid body blows and was heartily applauded it the end of the round. In the seventh round Haywood I became more cautious and no points were given or taken. Haywood in the eight round pressed, and with a right blow from the shoulder knocked Regan to the ropes. He, however, quickly recovered and lunging forward delivered a stinging blow on his opponent's ear. The ninth round saw Regan put in a h.Jivy neck blow but Haywood responded with a terrific body blow. I Regan, however, repelled all Haywood's other thrusts. The last round saw Haywood pressing strongly as it was his only chance. Regan received punishment before he could extricate himself from the corner but when the gong want both were sparring merrily. When quiet hqd been restored the referee announced that Haywood had won the contest by the tenth round. The loser however was anything bat disgriced. (Applause). Regan put up a good fight,and if his adversary had been a stone lighter the verdict might have been the other way. As it was, Regan was forced to defend practically all the time.

In the novices light weight' competition. open to soldiers and civillana in Warrington and radius or ten miles, there ;yes some spirited fighting particularly betwerm Harry Regan and Ted Tavlin, both of War- rington. Regan was declared the loser. Frank Ryan, and Herbert Hockenhall, both of Warrington, having passed through their heats met in the final. At the end of the second round Hockenhall showed signs of punish- ment. The third round saw Ryan administering heavy jabs on Hockenhall's face and body. Hockeahall was all but done when time was called. The win was of course awardei to Ryan who assuredly destirved it. In the competition of the middle weights was seen the only clean "knock-out" of the night, the contestants being Privates Murphy and Lewis. There was no scientific boxing in this contest, the two going ft.r it hammer and tongs. In the second round Murphy with a straight blow knocked Lewis almost through the ropes. On resuming Murphy with a powerful blow under the heart knocked Lewis clean out. Murphy and Private Byrne met in the final. Murphy had s6verely injured his right hand in the-above fight and was strongly advised not to cornpete.An old glove had to be procured for bim..lle was considerably hampered by this and was only able to defend and was completely at the mercy of Bryne. In the second round the referee stopped the _match, saying it was no use going on any further. Bryne therefore was recorded the winner.

In the light weight competition for soldiers only Privates Owen and Ely met in the final, Owen being victorious. In the featherweight competition there was a keen struggle between Privates Ellison and Shepherd. Ellison won on points, the referee saying it was a very good contest and he was sorry that he could not mike them both winners. The referee was Mr. Tom Gamble, selected by the "Sporting Chronicle." Captain D. 0. B. Dawson, of the South Lancashire Regiment acted as time keeper. Praise is due to the following committee for providing such excellent sport: —Captain Howard-Vyse, Captain Dawson,Captain M.H. B. Morgan, Lieutenant Nlarshall, Sergeant Major Scott, Sergeant Instructor linitht. The next meeting w:11 be on Thursday,November 4th.

A pretty wedding took place at Christ Church, Croft, when Mr. J. E. Sylvester, late of Croft, was m•arried to Miss Jessie Renshaw, daughter of Mr. John Henshaw, of Risley. The Rector, the Rev. H. I. Wadeson, officiated. PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

The best turn at the Palace and Hippodrome this week is "A 33 to 1 chance." To a sportsman it is especially entertaining. "A 33 to 1 chance" centres upon a boxing match which in due course cornea off between Jack Goldswain, the light-weight champion of England and Young Sullivan, the well-known American champion. In the first place Lord Stans- field (Mr. E Heaton Grey) makes a bet with Major C. Maine (Mr. Howard Templeton) that he will be respon- sible for bringing a man to beat young Sullivan. The bet made is 33 to 1 in thousands, and Maine noticing the confident manner of Lord Stansfield thinks that he does not stand to win so surely as he thought when he made the bet. Maine thinking that it must be Lord Stansfield himself who would box, and also knowing that if Lord Stansfield did not turn up at the fight the stakes will be his, he decides that it will be to his advantage to keep Lord Stansfield's money. Maine's ingeniousness is not at a loss to pre- vent Lord Stansfield's presence, and he deputes Solomon Levi (Mr. Fred Monti) a bookmaker, to pre- sent Lord Stansfield with a doctored cigar on his way to the club which would:sicken him and therefore he would not be able to be there. This ruse is successful, but things do not turn out as Maine desires them. Lord Stansfield had, so as to ensure a fight, sent a letter to the club, in case he did not present himself and this letter is opened and its contents state "if it is his misfortune not to be present he nominated his servant " Bobs " or Mr. Jack Goldswain." Things turn out as expected: Goldswaia defeats Sullivan and Lord Stansfield wins his bet. Massona, who is described on the programme as an "eccentric juggler," is really such—in fact he displays a• card which states that he is mad and he carries it out to the letter. Some tricks he performs are especially good. Rose and Calvert present themselves in a scene entitled "Captain Kettle and his charming mate," The extreinely excit- able nature of the captain and the urbanity of Roze commend themselves to everybody. The Floradoras, are three charming young ladies, who delight the audience with their singing and the scene of the lovers garden—one of them taking the part of a young man. Millie Howell, character vocalist, renders songs in a highly creditable manner. Arthur Woodville is rather pessimistic in his songs re the advantage of being single ' over married, but he retrieves himself later with his dancing. Mark Henton as a comedian is decidedly popular with the "gods." The Palace perfection pictures are very good and the orchestra under the conductorship of Mr. Widdop play some very enter- taining items. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

There is a capital" bill " for next week, the chief attraction being Sydney James' Strolling Players, including Gilbert Fernie, in their pastoral entertain- ment of mirth, music and mimicry. The other artistes will be :—Burley and Burley, the quaint acrobatic comedians ; Bruce Green, the popular talkative " dame " comedian; Maude Vera, comedienne and dancer; Gracie Graham, the popular comedienne; Austin and Cowan, vocal entertainers ; Lambert and Butler. comedians and dancers.

UNITED KINGDOM BAND OF HOPE AUTUMNAL CONFERENCE.

The 41st annual conference has been held at Cambridge during the past week when a large gather- ing of delegates from North, East, South and West, assembled. The proceedings opened on Saturday after- noon by a great children's demonstration. In the even- ing the delegates were formally welcomed to the town in the Guild Hall by his Worship the Mayor, Alderman W. P. Spalding, the chair being occupied by Professor G.Sirus Woodhead (president of the Cambridge Union). Other welcomes were given on behalf of the C.E.T.S. and Free Church Council and other local temperance bodies. Official sermons were preached on Sunday in St. Andrew's Church by the Dean of Carlisle and in Emmanuel Congregational Church by the Rev. Norman Macleod Caie, D.D., and in the evening at 8.15 a mass meeting was held in the Guild Hall which was presided over by Dr. Barker, headmaster of the Leys School, and addressed by Canon Barker, Rev., Dr. Stokes and others. On Monday the confereneeispenta busy day, papers being read by Mr. John Phillipson, Chief Ruler of 1.0. R. ; subject : "In what ways can the Beneficiary Element be made to assist Band of Hope Work ?" and by Mr. W. C.Bruce (Edinburgh) ; subject: " How to extend the sphere of operations of TONVII

Unions." The chair was occupied by Mr. Lionel Mundy who spoke of the work of the U.K.B.H. Union which now comprises 372 Band of Hope Unions con- sisting of 30,854 societies with a total of 3,765,000 members an army of young abstainers from which great things may be expected in coming years. A mostinter- esting discussion took placewhich brought the morning conference to a close. In the afternoon the delegates were taken through all the colleges which had been thrown open for their inspection, a privilege which was much enjoyed, and in the evening an official reception was held. On Tuesday morning the Conference was presided over by the Hon. Mrs. Elliott Yorke. Papers were read by Miss Annie Salmon, editor of the "Young. Abstainer," subject " Work among children untouched by Band of Hope Effort" and Mrs. J. A. Cotton on "Woman's place and power in Juvenile Temperance Work." In the evening a great demonstration was held in the Guild Hall which was crowded, Alderman John Chivers presiding and addresses being given by Rev. Norman Macleod Caie' D.D. (Edinburgh), and. Rev. W. Charles Pigott, both addresses dealing with the training of the children in habits of total abstinence. Another busy day was spent on ‘iednesday, when papers were given in the morning by Mr. Charles Wakely, secretary U.K.B.H.U. ; subject "The new school syllabus" and by Rev. Charles Wickham, subje-A "How can temperance teaching be suitably given in Sunday Schools. In the afternoon the delegates visited Ely where they were taken through the fine Cathedral and' afterwards a public meeting was held in the Town Hall. On Thursday a visit was paid to Bedford. The conference throughout has been noted for its enthusiasm, the workers in this cause being f ull of optimism.

THE

PIRATES OF PENZANCE,

Musical Society's Forthcoming Performances. INTERVIEWS WITH MR. F. H. CROSSLEY

AND MR. FODEN WILLIAMS.

Great interest is being taken by the music-loving public of Warrington in the seventh amateur operatic enterprise of the Warrington Musical Society.. The opportunity of witnessing the production of " The Pirates of Penzance," one of the most delightful and popular of the-famous Gilbert and Sullivan collalAra- tions, will doubtless be widely welcomed, and there is every indication that complete success will attend the performances in the Parr Hall during the week com- mencing October 11th. We understand that the rehearsals have been entered into with most commend- able enthusiasm, and it is confidently anticipated that the opera section will add another triumph to their highly creditable record.

"With the object of gleaning information as to the musical promise of this latest operatic venture, a representative of tho " Examiner " interviewed Mr. Frank H. Crossley, the conductor and musical director, who readily gave his impressions. First of all Crossley had something„inieresting to say about the character of the work selected for representation. "The Pirates of Penzance" he pointed out, was first produced in London in 1880 and followed " Pinafore " which had had the phenomenal run of two years. It is one of the most picturesque and amusing operas of the wonderful series which, resulted from the joint work of two such geniuses as Arthur Sullivan and W. S. Gilbert. It is rich in colour and contains a genuine and piquant story. Like "The Sorcerer" this was suggested by an allusion in one of the old "Bab Ballads" and was based on a characteristic Gilbertian idea—that of a band of pirates whose proceedings were regulated by a sort of topsy-turvy logic. A body of pirates naturally suggests other bodies who control them. Here was the author's opportunity for introducing the police—a topic handled with much humour., The passages dealing with the " force " are amongst the most amusing features of the piece, and the naYve expression of the policeman's emotions is quite delightful. The music supplies several opportunities for both principals and chorus to distinguish themselves. The finale of the first act and a scene early in the second 'act - in which all the ladies of the company are aissociated with the policemen—are worked up quite in the style of grand opera. There are several charming concerted numbers, two or three of which are very dramatic in their intensity. For absolute charm of melody and vocal simplicity it would be difficult to find anything finer than the lovely duet for Mabel and Frederic "Oh, leave me not," sung in the ruined abbey by moonlight. Referring to the forces engaged in the opera, Mr. Crossley said the greater number of the leading parts are being taken by members who have won their spurs in previous performances, but there are several new principals who will, he believes, achieve as great successes as their predecessors. The Society, he remarked, is decidedly fortunate in having such resources to call upon. As in previous years, Mr. Crossley spoke highly of the services of the orchestra which he pointed out is purely local in its composition, having no recourse to outside professional aid. "The Society" he con- tinued " may well be ,congratulated on having such an able body of instrumentalists who vie with the vocal members in the keenness and enthusiasm with which they enter into the really arduous preparations for the performances." Mr. Crossley bore unstinted testimony to the pains- taking and intelligent work of the whole of the opera company. "It is I think entirely due to their general loyalty and earnestness" he said, that the success of the previous productions has been so marked. The chorus is a fine body this year and the acting—under the capable coaching of Mr. Foden Williams—will be found most competent and full of realistic touches." As an example of this " realism," Mr. Crossley mentioned a laughable incident which occurred at the first joint rehearsal of the orchestra and vocal section. One striking scene in the opera is the capture of the ladies of the chorus by the pirates, and the fair prisoners give vent to their alarm by shrieking loudly. This was done with such startling effect at the rehearsal in question, that the majority of the members of the orchestra were quite upset and con- cerned ; indeed seine of the gentlemen rose from their places apparently with the intention of going to the rescue of the ladies. However, it was explained that the screaming was all part of the " business " of the opera, and the practice went on as smoothly as ever. Summing up his opinion of the prospects of the enterprise Mr. Crossley had no hesitation in declaring that, barring accidents, he believes the forthcoming performances will be a signal success in every rear ect.

===== 0002601_19091002_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
A Chat With Mr. Foden Williams.

For the third year in succession Mr. Foden Williams is fulfilling the important duties of coach and stage manager, and the Opera Committee is to be congratu- lated upon again securing the services of a gentleman of such wide experience and recognized ability in this class of work. Mr. Williams has coached many amateur societies in a variety of operas and it speaks well for the capabilitieß of the opera company of the Warring- ton Musical Society that he has formed sanguine opinions as to the promise of the forthcoming pm, duction.

"The Pirates of Penzance," Mr. Williams informed our representative, is an opera peculiarly suited to amateurs, in that it affords them ample opportunities for displaying their vocal powers without imposing a severe strain upon their acting and dancing abilities. "In no opera to my knowledge" said Mr. Williams " has Sir W. S. Gilbert shown a keener sense of satire. This is particularly manifest in the work of the policemen and I have no doubt that the audiences will derive much amusement from the say- ings and doings of these versatile representatives of law and order."

Giving his view of the forthcoming performances, as based upon the work done at the rehearsals, Mr. Williams said that everything points to the opera being a notable artistic success. The music is really charming and he will be surprised if the music lovers of Warrington are satisfied until they have heard again and again the beautiful unaccompanied choral singing in that gem of harmony" Hail Poetry." "And what is your opinion as to the abilities of the performers engaged in " The Pirates of Penzance" ? asked the interviewer.

"All the principals " replied Mr. Williams " show signs of scoring distinct successes, and not the least promising are the new aspirants." Mr. Williams also expressed himself in eulogistic terms with reference to the admirable work of the chorus. He explained that while dancing does not make the same heavy demand upon the performers as in "Morrie England "there is plenty of delicate movement which will appeal to the public. The hutnorous part of the opera is in capable hands and if the enjoyment at the rehearsals is any criterion, Mr. Williams has no doubt that the audiences will find a great deal to arouse their merriment as well as to elevate and interest them.

"All the principals " concluded Mr. Williams "have proved themselves to be exceedingly apt pupils and lam most hopeful that the performances will be fully in keeping with the high reputation of the opera company.'
